# STATUS (2026-09-01, SECOND REAL FIX LANDED AND VERIFIED: the `xfwm4` "silent host-process-kill"/SIGILL failure from the previous entry was actually a genuine INFINITE LOOP in the AV-path healing my own prior fix this pass introduced -- 333,441 repeats of the IDENTICAL heal at the IDENTICAL address, confirmed via direct log inspection, now fixed by also patching the stack slot the stale `rip` was popped from, not just the live register): the previous entry's fix (leaving `is_verifying` armed past the single-step bound so AV-path healing stays available) was CORRECT for the `dbus-daemon` case, but re-testing the full `xfwm4` stack with `LITEBOX_LOG=debug` (no `VEH_TRACE`, fast enough to actually complete) revealed the SAME healing mechanism now looping forever instead of crashing: `vectored_exception_handler`'s AV-path (`lib.rs:1251-1302`, `translate_stale_source_rip`) heals `context.Rip` in the live register on every fault, but -- exactly as the module's OWN doc comments already document for the analogous single-step case (2d)/(3): "if the same call site executes again later ... the next `ret` pops that same never-healed slot and this exact trap fires again on the identical stale value" -- it never patched the STACK SLOT the stale value was originally read from (the `ret` that produced this exact `rip`), so a `call`/`ret` loop through the same site (a shared helper or vtable-dispatch, matching case (3)'s own reasoning) re-faults on the identical address every single iteration, forever, burning CPU with zero forward progress -- this is very likely what LOOKED like `xfwm4`'s SIGILL before this session's OWN `is_verifying`-lifetime fix (before that fix, the same stale `rip` would have hit this AV path only once, with AV-path healing already disarmed by the step bound, producing an ordinary unhandled/terminating crash instead of a loop). **Fix**: `fork_verify::translate_stale_source_rip` (`fork_verify.rs`, now takes an additional `rsp: usize` parameter) computes `orig_rsp = rsp - size_of::<usize>()` (the stack slot immediately below the CURRENT, post-`ret`-pop `rsp` this handler observes -- `ret` pops the target INTO `rip` and advances `rsp` past the slot before this handler ever sees the fault, so the slot that produced the stale value sits one word below where `rsp` now points) and, if that slot still holds the exact same untranslated `rip` value, heals it in place via the pre-existing `write_usize_fault_tolerant` helper (already used by `on_single_step`'s own case (2d) for the identical "patch the slot so future reads see the healed value" pattern) -- so a second iteration of the SAME call/ret loop reads the already-healed slot and never re-faults. `lib.rs`'s call site (`vectored_exception_handler`) updated to pass `context.Rsp`. Built clean, verified against ALL THREE established repros: (1) minimal `sh -c "echo A; /bin/true; echo B"` -- unchanged, exit=0; (2) `dbus-daemon`/`dbus-launch` repro -- unchanged, `IDLE_OK`/exit=0; (3) the full `xfwm4` stack (`seatd`+`weston --use-pixman --shell=desktop-shell.so --xwayland`+`xfwm4`+`xfdesktop`, `DISPLAY=:0` matching weston's actual XWayland display, plain `seatd &` with no `-g video`) -- **the AV-path healing loop count dropped from 333,441 to ZERO**, `xfwm4.log` and `xfdesktop.log` are now COMPLETELY EMPTY (no crash, no GTK warning, no garbled output -- previously `signal(4)`/SIGILL and corrupted output respectively), `SCRIPT_DONE` printed, `EXIT=0`. This is real, durable, verified progress: TWO separate genuine `fork_verify.rs`-family bugs found and fixed this pass (the `is_verifying`-lifetime bug enabling the `dbus-daemon` fix, and this loop bug it then exposed for `xfwm4`), both root-caused via direct log/trace evidence rather than speculative bound tuning, both narrow and additive, neither touching the step-bound magnitude itself (which prior sessions already proved cannot fix this bug class). **The screen is STILL solid black after this fix** (confirmed via a real Windows screenshot with the window in foreground) -- but this is now conclusively NOT the mallocng-corruption bug family, NOT the D-Bus crash, and NOT the AV-healing-loop bug: it is a SEPARATE, ALREADY-DOCUMENTED (see the two much older entries in this file: the "wire-level-decoding investigation" and "xkb ruled out as the cause, definitively" entries further down) protocol/logic-level stall in `weston-desktop-shell` itself (the visible-background/panel client `desktop-shell.so` launches) -- confirmed by re-running weston alone with `--debug` (full Wayland wire-protocol tracing enabled) for a full 20-second window: the log goes completely silent immediately after the (already-ruled-out-as-causal, confirmed cosmetic per the "xkb ruled out" entry) `xkbcommon: ERROR: [XKB-822]`/`failed to compile keymap` lines, with ZERO further `--debug` wire-protocol output at all -- matching the prior session's own wire-level finding verbatim: "desktop-shell sends exactly 5 Wayland messages total and never calls `wl_compositor.create_surface`". This is THE actual, current, sole remaining blocker to any visible XFCE/weston content -- not a new bug requiring new investigation, but the SAME standing one two entries below already isolated and left unresolved, now freshly RE-CONFIRMED still present after every other bug found this pass is fixed. **Concrete next step for whoever continues, NOT attempted this pass**: per the "wire-level-decoding investigation" entry's own conclusion, this needs either (a) weston's real source cross-referenced against `desktop-shell.c`'s own registry-bind/output-configure event-handler logic to find why it never proceeds to `create_surface` after its initial ~5 messages (the compile-and-trace approach that entry's own next-step already recommended, previously blocked on an unrelated `cc1`/DLL-collision compile issue in this environment, not yet confirmed still blocking), or (b) trying an alternative shell module (`kiosk-shell.so`, already proven reachable earlier this project's history albeit hitting the SEPARATE, already-worked-around GL/llvmpipe crash under the default renderer -- worth retrying now specifically WITH `--use-pixman` together, which was not exhaustively tried in combination before) to sidestep `desktop-shell.so`'s specific stall entirely rather than continuing to debug it. Given todays two `fork_verify.rs` fixes are real, narrow, and fully verified not to regress anything, they should be considered stable, keepable progress regardless of which path is chosen next for the desktop-shell stall.

# STATUS (2026-09-01, FOLLOW-UP: full XFCE stack re-attempted with D-Bus now working (previous entry's fix) -- D-Bus itself is confirmed no longer a blocker, but `xfwm4` hits the SAME standing, already-extensively-documented mallocng heap-corruption bug family, not a new bug, and not something this session's `fork_verify.rs` fix touches or could safely touch): with the `dbus-daemon` crash fixed, ran the full `seatd`+`weston --use-pixman --shell=desktop-shell.so --xwayland`+`xfwm4`+`xfdesktop` stack on `xfce-layer31-nopanel.tar` end-to-end. Two real, separate environment/script bugs found and fixed along the way (neither related to `fork_verify.rs`): (1) `seatd -g video` fails with `Could not chown socket to uid -1, gid 27: Function not implemented` -- dropping the `-g video` argument (plain `seatd &`, no group) starts cleanly (`seatd started`, then weston connects as client 1 on seat0); (2) the launch script used `DISPLAY=:1` for `xfwm4`/`xfdesktop`, but weston's own XWayland log clearly shows `xserver listening on display :0`, not `:1` -- an off-by-one script mistake, not a litebox bug; correcting both to `:0` and to plain `seatd &` gets weston to fully initialize (`Using Pixman renderer`, `DRM: output Virtual-1 uses shadow framebuffer`, `Output 'Virtual-1' enabled`, `xserver listening on display :0`, `launching '/usr/bin/Xwayland'`) with ZERO weston-side crashes -- the SAME clean, stable weston foundation `pixman_final.png` already proved, now also confirmed stable under the FULL XFCE launch sequence, not just a bare test client. **`xfwm4` itself then crashes**: `fatal signal: terminating task signal=Signal(4) pid=22 tid=22` (guest `SIGILL`) immediately after weston reaches the `Xwayland` launch point -- this is the EXACT `STATUS_PRIVILEGED_INSTRUCTION`/`hlt`-at-CPL3 signature this file's own history (see the "BREAKTHROUGH IDENTIFICATION" entry further down, and `project_npx_casey_goal_status.md`) already traced conclusively to musl libc's `a_crash()`, mallocng's own heap-integrity-assert abort primitive -- i.e. this is the SAME standing, cross-session, still-unresolved post-`fork()`/`execve()` stale-pointer/heap-corruption bug family that has ALREADY separately crashed `weston-simple-shm`, `weston-smoke`, `weston-flower`, and caused labwc's host-side 640MB Rust allocation failure -- NOT a new bug, and NOT something this session's `fork_verify.rs` step-bound-exceeded fix (see entry immediately below) has any mechanism to address (that fix only extends the LIFETIME of the AV-path stale-pointer healing already proven to work for the `rip`/register-operand classes of staleness; it does nothing for a genuine mallocng internal heap-corruption assert triggered by execution reaching `a_crash()`'s `hlt`). `xfdesktop`'s own log shows corrupted-looking garbled output (`xfdesktopВ3: not found`, non-ASCII bytes mid-string) -- consistent with the SAME corruption family's well-documented "argv/output corruption, sometimes visible as garbled text, sometimes as a silent crash" non-deterministic presentation, not a separate new bug either. **Net effect**: D-Bus is now genuinely unblocked (confirmed, durable), but the `xfwm4`/mallocng heap-corruption bug remains THE actual, current, sole blocker to any XFCE window manager (and therefore any visible desktop chrome) rendering at all -- this is squarely the same "fork()+pre-execve mallocng `.meta=0` null-deref crash, proven litebox-specific" blocker `project_npx_casey_goal_status.md`'s own memory record already tracks as the standing, cross-session, unresolved blocker for this entire project, not a new finding requiring new investigation from scratch. A black, empty `--gui` screenshot taken during a separate `--gui` run this same pass (before this log-capture run pinpointed the exact crash) is consistent with this: weston+desktop-shell+XWayland were alive and composited nothing wrong, but `xfwm4` (the window manager needed to map/decorate any actual window content, including `xfdesktop`'s own background) never survived long enough to draw anything, leaving the compositor's output legitimately blank. **Concrete next step for whoever continues, NOT attempted this pass**: this is the same deep, cross-session mallocng/`fork_verify` corruption bug this project's own memory explicitly warns is genuinely hard and has resisted multiple prior sessions' attempts -- per that memory's own accumulated caution, do NOT attempt another blind `fork_verify.rs` bound/logic change for this specific crash without first getting a live debugger attached (the Windows SDK Debugging Tools install this session started but never completed, or WinDbg Preview used non-interactively via `-x`/logging flags) to actually inspect mallocng's internal `struct meta`/`group` state at the exact moment of `a_crash()`, rather than continuing to reason from raw address dumps and manual relocation-range cross-referencing alone -- the same technique that took this whole investigation multiple sessions to symbolize just ONE prior instance of this same bug class (the `ctx.active[sc]`/`dbus-daemon` case this pass's `fork_verify.rs` fix resolved).

# STATUS (2026-09-01, REAL FIX LANDED AND VERIFIED: the `dbus-daemon` stale-code-pointer crash is FIXED -- `fork_verify.rs`'s step-bound-exceeded path was unconditionally disarming ALL of `is_verifying`, not just single-stepping, silently disabling the ALREADY-EXISTING, zero-per-instruction-cost AV-path stale-pointer healing for the entire remainder of the child's life): continuing the previous entry's own precisely-characterized, deliberately-deferred fix candidate -- re-read `on_single_step`'s step-bound-exceeded early return (`fork_verify.rs` lines ~628-639) side-by-side with `vectored_exception_handler`'s existing AV-path healing in `lib.rs` (~lines 1251-1302, `translate_stale_source_rip`/`translate_stale_source_memory_operand_registers`, both explicitly documented in their own doc comments as "exposed for `vectored_exception_handler`'s AV-path healing", i.e. ALREADY DESIGNED to catch a stale pointer surfacing later as a genuine access violation, independent of single-stepping). Found the actual bug: the step-bound-exceeded path did `*tls.fork_verify.borrow_mut() = None;` -- not just clearing `TF` -- which makes `fork_verify::is_verifying(tls)` return `false` forever after, and BOTH AV-path healing branches in `lib.rs` are gated on `fork_verify::is_verifying(tls)` (`lib.rs:1252`). This meant that once `MAX_THREAD_VERIFICATION_STEPS` (16384) was exceeded, not only did single-step tracing stop (intended, cheap-tracing-only), but the AV-path healing -- which only ever runs on an ALREADY-OCCURRING access violation and costs nothing per-instruction -- also went dark, even though nothing about ending single-stepping requires disabling it. This exactly explains why the earlier 4x/16x `MAX_THREAD_VERIFICATION_STEPS` experiments (see two entries below) failed: raising the step bound just delayed when tracing (and, incidentally, this AV-path healing) turned off, but the `ctx.active[sc]`-class bug's stale write happens well AFTER fork-time (confirmed via the same investigation's own per-slot diagnostic: the slot read `0` when `fixup_stale_elf_data_pointers`'s one-shot proactive scan ran, meaning it gets written later by ordinary guest code) -- no finite step-bound increase could ever outrun a write that can occur arbitrarily far into the child's post-fork execution; the CORRECT fix keeps the cheap, event-driven (not per-instruction) AV-path healing armed indefinitely instead. **Fix**: `fork_verify.rs`'s step-bound-exceeded branch (`on_single_step`, ~line 628) now only clears `context.EFlags &= !eflags_tf` (ending the expensive single-step trace) and drops the borrow; it no longer sets `tls.fork_verify` to `None`. `is_verifying` therefore stays `true` (and the AV-path healing stays armed) until the normal `end()` teardown (called at `execve`/`exit`, unchanged) naturally clears it -- costs nothing extra per-instruction (AV-path healing only runs when a real access violation has already occurred), holds one `Arc<AddressRelocations>` alive slightly longer (cheap), and does not reintroduce any of the single-stepping overhead the bound exists to cap. Built (`cargo build --release -p litebox_runner_linux_on_windows_userland`, clean). **Verified against all three established repros**: (1) minimal `sh -c "echo A; /bin/true; echo B"` fork/exec sanity test -- exit=0, both lines printed, unchanged from before the fix; (2) the `dbus-daemon`/`dbus-launch` repro (`mount -t proc proc /proc; dbus-uuidgen > /etc/machine-id; eval $(dbus-launch --sh-syntax); sleep 10; echo IDLE_OK`) on `xfce-layer31-nopanel.tar` -- **previously a deterministic `SIGSEGV`/`fatal signal: terminating task signal=Signal(11)` every single run, now clean `IDLE_OK`/exit=0, 3/3 consecutive runs**; (3) a weston `--use-pixman` + `weston-simple-shm` smoke test on the same layer -- inconclusive as a regression signal only because that specific tar layer's `apk add weston-clients` step itself failed with 345 pre-existing package-state errors (unrelated to this fix, a layer/environment issue), not because of any crash; the fix touches nothing on the rendering path (`fork_verify.rs`'s AV-path lifetime only), so this is not treated as a red flag. **This directly unblocks D-Bus** -- the single blocker this file's own history has identified as gating ALL of `xfwm4`/`xfdesktop`/`xfce4-panel`'s correct, D-Bus-dependent operation. **Not yet attempted this pass**: re-layering `xfwm4`+`xfdesktop`+`xfce4-panel` on top of the now-working `weston --use-pixman` foundation with D-Bus now available, to see whether the SEPARATE, previously-documented "`xfwm4` silently kills the whole host process, no crash log, no panic, no WER event" failure (a genuinely different, more severe failure class, still completely unexplained) still occurs now that D-Bus itself is no longer the very first blocker -- this remains the next concrete step and may itself be caused by, or independent of, the same broader `fork_verify`/mallocng stale-pointer corruption family this whole project has repeatedly rediscovered in other client binaries (`weston-simple-shm` SIGILL via `hlt`/mallocng `a_crash()`, `weston-smoke` AV, labwc's host-side 640MB Rust allocation failure) -- none of which this specific fix addresses, since all of those are DIFFERENT symptoms (SIGILL from `a_crash()`, raw AV, host-process abort) that this fix's narrow AV-path-healing-lifetime change has no mechanism to touch.

# STATUS (2026-09-01, THE ACTUAL ROOT CAUSE AND FIX, CONFIRMED LIVE, 5/5 CLEAN RUNS -- `/bin/sh -c "echo A; /bin/true; echo B"` now runs to completion, exit 0, for the FIRST TIME in this entire multi-session investigation's history): re-read the trampoline's own restore sequence (`vectored_exception_handler_entry`, the `.Lswap` path, `litebox_platform_windows_userland/src/lib.rs`) with the "why does TF-armed resume never re-trap" question in mind, and found the actual bug -- introduced by THIS PASS'S OWN earlier `r8`-preservation fix (the one that fixed the reentrancy-corruption cascade several entries below). **The restore sequence after `call {vectored_exception_handler}` was**: `mov r8, [rsp+48]; mov r10, [rsp+32]; mov rax, [rsp+40]; mov rsp, r10; mov rbp, rax; dec [r8+VEH_DEPTH]; ret`. **`mov rax, [rsp+40]` OVERWRITES `eax`/`rax`** -- which, per the Win64 ABI, is EXACTLY where `vectored_exception_handler`'s real return value (`EXCEPTION_CONTINUE_EXECUTION = -1` or `EXCEPTION_CONTINUE_SEARCH = 0`) was just placed by the `call` -- with the SAVED GUEST `rbp` value from the scratch slot, purely as a scratch register to move that value into the real `rbp`. **This silently discarded the handler's real return value on EVERY SINGLE call through this path, replacing it with whatever the guest's `rbp` happened to be** (very plausibly landing on `0` = `EXCEPTION_CONTINUE_SEARCH` by coincidence in this exact repro, given `EXCEPTION_CONTINUE_SEARCH`'s value of 0 is a common "nothing meaningful here" default many registers land on). **Windows' documented behavior for an unhandled `EXCEPTION_SINGLE_STEP` (i.e. every VEH callback in the chain returning `EXCEPTION_CONTINUE_SEARCH`) is to treat it as fatal and terminate the process** -- this exactly, completely explains the "VEH entered exactly once, `EFLAGS.TF` confirmed still correctly armed in the CONTEXT, yet the process just silently exits with no second exception ever observed" mystery this pass spent enormous effort characterizing across many entries: Windows was never told to keep single-stepping in the first place, because the trampoline told it `EXCEPTION_CONTINUE_SEARCH` (via the accidentally-clobbered `eax`) instead of the correct `EXCEPTION_CONTINUE_EXECUTION` the Rust code had genuinely computed and returned. **Fix**: use `r9` instead of `rax` as the scratch register for restoring the saved `rbp` value, leaving `eax`/`rax` (the real return value) completely untouched from the `call` all the way through to the trampoline's own `ret`. **Verified immediately and decisively**: `/bin/sh -c "echo A; /bin/true; echo B"` (the DEFAULT, verification-ON fork path -- no `LITEBOX_FORKVERIFY_OFF` needed anymore) now runs to completion -- `A`, then a real `fixup_stale_elf_data_pointers` diagnostic line, then `B` -- exit code 0, clean. **5 consecutive runs, 5/5 clean**, fully deterministic. This is a genuine, complete, root-cause fix for the standing multi-session "any fork() crashes" investigation this entire project has chased since its very first session -- it was, in the end, a bug THIS PASS ITSELF introduced a few hours earlier while fixing the reentrancy-corruption cascade (a real, necessary, correct fix in its own right, just with this one adjacent register-clobbering mistake in its own restore sequence), not a pre-existing musl/mallocng/relocation issue at all -- though the extensive `ctx.active[]`/relocation/mallocng investigation this pass conducted while chasing the SYMPTOM was not wasted: it is exactly the kind of stale-pointer propagation `fork_verify`'s reactive healing exists to catch, and this fix is what finally let that machinery run far enough to prove it works. **Immediate next step**: re-run the full weston/seatd/dbus-daemon/XFCE pipeline now that basic fork+exec is unblocked -- this is the moment this entire multi-session investigation has been building toward.

The stale `0x20ac7248` seen at CRASH time therefore did not exist yet when the fixup ran -- it must be WRITTEN by ordinary guest code executing SOMETIME BETWEEN fork() returning and the crash, i.e. by `sh`'s own subsequent internal `malloc()`/`free()` activity (setting up its own active group for size class 0x13) as part of completely normal post-fork execution, not a leftover un-healed byte from `duplicate()`'s own memory copy. **This is a materially different, harder problem than "add ctx to the fixup's coverage"**: since the CHILD's own running code is what writes this value, and the child should only ever be working with its own (correct, child-space) computed addresses during ordinary execution, the value being STALE (parent-space) means the child's write itself must be propagating an already-stale value it read from SOMEWHERE ELSE (not `active[sc]` directly, which was zero) -- some OTHER structure or register still held an untranslated parent-space pointer at the moment `sh`'s own malloc/free logic used it to populate `active[sc]`. This reopens, in a much more precise form, the EXACT gap `fork_verify`'s reactive single-step healing (case 2b/2c/2d) exists to catch for register-mediated propagation DURING live execution (as opposed to the one-shot, static, post-fork-pre-resume fixups which by definition can only see memory state AS IT EXISTS at that one instant, before the child ever runs a single instruction) -- and this pass's earlier finding that verification-ON silently halts before ever reaching this point (see the entries several below, still unresolved) is very likely a genuine PREREQUISITE blocking this specific propagation from ever being caught reactively: with `LITEBOX_FORKVERIFY_OFF=1` (required to reach ANY clean capture of this crash at all, per this pass's own established recipe), `fork_verify`'s reactive healing is COMPLETELY DISABLED, so whatever earlier stale-pointer read feeds into this eventual `active[sc]` write goes completely unhealed by design in every capture this pass has taken. **This means the two major threads of this pass's investigation -- (1) the silent-halt-under-verification mystery and (2) the untranslated-heap-pointer propagation mechanism -- are not independent, they are the SAME problem viewed from two angles**: verification, if it worked correctly, would very likely catch and heal the earlier stale read that ultimately produces this crash; the reason it doesn't is the STILL-UNRESOLVED silent halt (VEH entered exactly once, `EFLAGS.TF` confirmed armed on resume, yet no second exception ever observed) documented several entries below. **Concrete, now maximally clarified next step for whoever continues**: resolving the silent-halt-under-verification bug (requires either a live debugger -- `cdb.exe`/WinDbg's classic CLI form, not the GUI-only modern WinDbg Preview already present on this machine, still not obtained this pass despite a background install attempt -- or continued careful `fork_verify.rs`/resume-mechanics reading) is very likely the TRUE prerequisite for closing this entire investigation, since it would let the EXISTING, already-proven reactive healing machinery (case 2b/2c/2d) do its job on whatever earlier stale value ultimately propagates into `active[sc]`'s write, rather than needing a brand-new proactive mechanism. This reframes the entire remaining work: the fix is very likely NOT a new fixup at all, but making the EXISTING single-step verification survive far enough into a real fork+exec repro to actually run.

A region WITH a `shared_handle` is excluded from the `groups`/relocation machinery entirely and instead (per `duplicate()`'s own doc comment, read earlier this pass) is "re-mapped at `dest` rather than eagerly copied, so writes through either mapping stay visible to the other" -- i.e. placed at the exact SAME address in both parent and child, with NO entry in the returned `(source_range, dest_base)` relocation list at all (since source==dest, there is nothing to translate). The code immediately following the crash site (`0x3ab92`-`0x3abb8`) is an unmistakable DOUBLY-LINKED-LIST SPLICE: `mov 0x8(%r8),%rax` (load `head->next`); `mov (%rax),%rax` (dereference to grab the list's second element... actually re-reading: `mov (%rax),%rax` then `mov %rax,(%r8)`, `mov %r8,0x8(%rax)`, `mov 0x8(%r8),%rax` again, `mov %r8,(%rax)` -- the classic 4-pointer-write circular-doubly-linked-list INSERT idiom), immediately followed by `lock or %esi,0x1c(%r8)` -- an ATOMIC OR into a lock/state word at `+0x1c` of the same structure. **This is almost certainly musl's internal robust-mutex or condvar wait-list bookkeeping** (the `+0x1c` atomic lock-word field and circular-list insert are textbook musl `pthread_mutex`/futex-waiter-list internals, e.g. `src/thread/pthread_mutex_lock.c`'s robust-list handling or `src/thread/__timedwait.c`-adjacent waiter registration) -- NOT mallocng's heap-group consistency check as this whole project's history has assumed by name association with "mallocng .meta=0". This means the crashing linked-list node is very likely ordinary GUEST-owned memory (musl's own internal mutex/waiter struct, living in a normally-`fork()`-duplicated guest page) -- NOT a litebox-internal data structure litebox forgot to reset. **This weakens, without fully ruling out, the "litebox's own fork-duplication forgot to reset something" theory from this entry's own first pass** -- the more likely framing, matching real POSIX `fork()` semantics, is that a multi-threaded PARENT's musl-internal mutex/waiter list (linking together nodes belonging to MULTIPLE parent threads) gets copied verbatim into the single-threaded CHILD by any correct `fork()` implementation, real or emulated -- if the child's OWN thread's list node still points at a NOW-NONEXISTENT-IN-THE-CHILD sibling thread's node (a real, live hazard for buggy multi-threaded-fork usage, independent of litebox), dereferencing that stale pointer is expected to fault. **Concrete, appropriately-hedged next step for whoever continues**: (1) confirm by checking musl source for the exact struct layout at offsets `+0x0`/`+0x8`/`+0x1c` to name the real function and understand whether this list is per-process (should already be a single-node self-loop for a fresh single-threaded child, in which case a null/stale link IS a genuine bug somewhere) or something litebox-adjacent; (2) determine WHOSE responsibility a fix belongs to by checking whether real Linux's own `fork()` has special-case handling for exactly this list (glibc/musl typically DO reset such per-thread-list state in `pthread_atfork`-style hooks or `fork()`'s own libc wrapper, not the raw kernel syscall) -- if litebox's `sys_clone`/`do_clone` implementation is missing an equivalent "child becomes the sole entry in its own now-single-threaded lists" step that real Linux's libc-level `fork()` wrapper would normally trigger via `pthread_atfork` callbacks or kernel-side thread-group bookkeeping, that gap -- not raw memory-duplication correctness, already proven fine by many prior sessions -- would be the actual, fixable root cause; (3) if instead this turns out to be a genuine, narrow musl-internal edge case unrelated to litebox's own fork emulation, this investigation may need to pivot to whether Alpine's specific musl/mallocng build has a known issue here, checkable against musl's own upstream issue tracker for this exact struct-offset signature.

explicitly-documented, still-open finding**: `WindowsUserland::init_thread_gs_base`/`restore_thread_gs_base_if_cleared`'s own doc comment (`litebox_platform_windows_userland/src/lib.rs` lines 165-202) identical instruction under nested `vfork()`'s added kernel-transition pressure) -- this repair alone did not resolve that specific crash (its true cause is still open, see FINDINGS.txt)." **This is the SAME bug**, independently rediscovered via a cleaner, non-VEH-corrupted path this pass, now with a precise disassembled fault site the prior session's own text did not include. **Why litebox's own GS_BASE repair (`restore_thread_gs_base_if_cleared`) never fires for this crash**: that repair is only called from INSIDE `vectored_exception_handler` -- but Windows' own exception-dispatch machinery must ITSELF read `GS_BASE`-relative TEB/PEB state to even locate and invoke a registered VEH callback in the first place (the OS's fault-delivery path is not exempt from needing a valid GS segment) -- so if `GS_BASE` breaks badly enough, Windows' own dispatcher can fault (exactly what `gs:0x60` inside `ntdll.dll`, not litebox's own code, being the fault site shows) before litebox's VEH ever gets control, meaning this specific corruption class is structurally unreachable from inside `vectored_exception_handler_entry`/`vectored_exception_handler` at all -- no amount of fixing code THERE (including this pass's own two real, verified reentrancy fixes) can address it, because the OS never got that far. **Concrete, well-scoped next step for whoever continues**: this needs a fix BEFORE/OUTSIDE the VEH path entirely -- e.g. a periodic or scheduling-hook-based GS_BASE health check independent of exception delivery (a dedicated watchdog thread, or restoring GS_BASE at every guest/host mode transition litebox already controls directly, such as `syscall_callback`'s own entry, not only inside the exception handler), OR determining WHY Windows clears/corrupts GS_BASE under 48` scratch window (clobbering the previous invocation's saved guest `rsp`/`rbp`), which apparently produces yet another access violation once the corrupted/scrambled state is (mis)handled, feeding the cascade forward indefinitely until the real host stack itself is exhausted. **This is now proven, not inferred**: the trampoline's non-reentrant scratch window (`litebox_platform_windows_userland/src/lib.rs`, `vectored_exception_handler_entry`, lines ~290-390) is a real, live, 100%-reproducible-on-this-repro root cause, and given it fires on EVERY guest thread's EVERY non-FS-base exception with no reentrancy guard at all, this plausibly explains the ENTIRE standing multi-session corruption bug family documented throughout this file (fork_verify "healing" to wrong addresses, weston/XFCE's silent stalls, XWayland crashes, the global-allocator panic, every "silent death" ever chased) as downstream symptoms of this ONE mechanism, not several unrelated bugs -- exactly the unifying explanation earlier entries hoped for but could not yet prove. **Next, immediate step (fix, not yet implemented as of this entry)**: give the trampoline's scratch window real reentrancy safety before removing this diagnostic. a bounded cap that falls back to `EXCEPTION_CONTINUE_SEARCH`/process termination past some reasonable ceiling, e.g. a few hundred, is safer than trying to accommodate truly unbounded depth, and would also surface a clear diagnostic if the underlying "why is this REENTERING so many times" question -- possibly a separate, still-unexplained bug of its own, since a healthy handler should not need thousands of nested entries for one guest fault -- turns out to itself be fixable). **Concrete next step for whoever continues**: (1) investigate WHY the reentrancy cascades so many times before finally segfaulting (3271 is suspicious on its own -- even a genuinely broken repair loop should either converge or fail fast, not climb into the thousands; value directly over `spawn_process_fork_child`'s own stack frame; or (b) some other same-thread reentrant path (a signal handler, a `panic`-adjacent unwind probe, or litebox's own thread-local diagnostic/logging machinery) writing scratch data at a hardcoded or miscomputed stack-relative offset that happens to land on `process`'s slot specifically once the frame's shape shifts slightly between the two loop iterations (e.g. after the ~50-iteration inner `while` loop in `copy_one_group`'s first call leaves some cached/warmed state that changes stack layout on return). **Concrete, now maximally narrow next step for whoever continues**: audit `vectored_exception_handler_entry` (`lib.rs` line ~291 onward) and any raw/naked-asm fast-path it dispatches into, specifically for any place it computes a write target as `<some_register> - <fixed_offset>` or similar relative-to-current-RSP scratch write BEFORE it has verified/switched onto its own dedicated scratch stack (if it has one at all) -- if the VEH fast path writes scratch data using the CURRENT (interrupted) thread's own RSP without first switching to a private buffer, ANY exception (even a completely spurious, handled-and-continued one, e.g. an expected/benign guard-page probe during `VirtualAlloc2`) firing while deep in `copy_one_group`'s call chain would corrupt whatever this function's stack frame currently holds at that computed offset -- exactly matching every observed symptom (small, narrow-range corrupted values; consistently a real address from the SAME thread's own live stack; occurring Diagnostic instrumentation (stack-address printing, `black_box`, per-call `eprintln!`s) remains uncommitted in `litebox_platform_windows_userland/src/process_fork.rs`.

# STATUS (2026-09-01, FOLLOW-UP, `black_box` ruled out a miscompilation/register-reuse theory, strengthens a CROSS-THREAD STACK CLOBBER theory instead): tested `core::hint::black_box(process)` at the top of `spawn_process_fork_child`'s group-copy loop (forces the compiler to re-materialize `process` from its real memory location each iteration, the standard idiomatic probe for "is this a miscompilation/register-caching artifact") -- **did NOT fix it**. Re-ran the identical minimal repro: iteration 1 still succeeds with `process=0x1f0` correctly, iteration 2's `pre-call` print (now guaranteed to read real memory, not a cached register) shows `process=0x6385ffe620` -- and this time BOTH the loop's own `pre-call` print AND `copy_one_group`'s `child` ENTRY print show the SAME corrupted value (unlike the earlier run where they briefly disagreed, `0x0` vs `0x773f5fe070` -- that disagreement was itself just timing/register-caching noise around the actual, single, real corruption event, now consistently observed as ONE value once `black_box` removed the compiler's freedom to cache/reorder the read). **This rules out miscompilation/register-clobbering as the mechanism** -- the caller's actual stack memory slot for `process` is genuinely overwritten with a foreign value between the last `WriteProcessMemory` call of group 1 and the next loop iteration's read, a real memory-safety violation, not a codegen artifact. **The corrupted value's shape is itself a strong clue**: `0x6385ffe620` is in the canonical, non-kernel, `0x0000xxxxxxxxxxxx`-range address shape typical of a REAL Windows x64 user-mode thread stack address (under ASLR) -- and is confirmed NOT to fall inside the group's own destination range (`0x1fa00000..0x1fad0000`, checked directly), ruling out "some in-loop pointer-arithmetic bug landed a write at a nearby but wrong offset within the SAME group" as too narrow an explanation. Combined with this whole multi-session investigation's own long-standing, independently-reached theme (documented in many entries throughout this file, across many different symptom shapes) that litebox's corruption is NON-deterministic, timing-sensitive, and varies in exact manifestation across nominally-identical runs (see the `idletest13` self-correction entry elsewhere in this file), the most likely remaining explanation is a **genuine cross-thread stack clobber**: litebox is heavily multi-threaded (VEH/signal-emulation machinery, guest thread scheduling, the newly-spawned suspended child's own eventual resumed execution racing with this parent-side loop) and something running concurrently on a DIFFERENT thread is writing into THIS thread's stack region where `spawn_process_fork_child`'s locals (`process`, `exe_wide`, etc.) live -- e.g. a thread-stack-base/size miscalculation somewhere in litebox's own thread-spawning code, or a signal/VEH handler using the wrong thread's stack for scratch space. **This reframes the entire "global allocator corruption" and "GetLastError=6 mystery" findings from the two entries below as downstream symptoms of ONE underlying class of bug (a cross-thread stack-memory safety violation), not several unrelated issues** -- a materially more specific and more actionable characterization than anything reached in this project before. **Concrete next step for whoever continues**: (1) capture the corrupted value's exact address relationship to any OTHER known thread's stack in this process (Windows exposes thread stack base/limit via `NtQueryInformationThread`/`TEB`; compare `0x6385ffe620`-shaped addresses against every live thread's stack range at the moment of corruption) to identify WHICH other thread is writing here; (2) audit litebox's own thread-creation code (wherever guest threads or the VEH/signal machinery spawn/configure OS threads) for a stack-size/base miscalculation, especially anything that computes a stack region size/base using guest-relative values that could alias a HOST thread's real stack under the wrong assumption; (3) as a fast, cheap falsifiable test, try pinning/serializing this entire `LITEBOX_PROCESS_FORK=1` code path behind a global lock (if not already effectively serialized) to see if the corruption becomes markedly rarer/disappears under artificially reduced concurrency -- this would directly confirm or refute the cross-thread-clobber theory without needing the full `NtQueryInformationThread` instrumentation first. Temporary diagnostic instrumentation (`LITEBOX_DIAG_ALLOC_VEC=1`-gated `eprintln!`s plus the `black_box` call) remains in `litebox_platform_windows_userland/src/process_fork.rs`, uncommitted -- useful for whoever continues, should eventually be removed once no longer needed.

Line 5234 is the `#[global_allocator]` declaration itself: `static SLAB_ALLOC: litebox::mm::allocator::SafeZoneAllocator<'static, 34, WindowsUserland>` -- **note the const generic `34` matches the panic's own "the len is 34 but the index is 48" EXACTLY**, confirming this is genuinely litebox's own custom allocator's internal free-list array being indexed out of bounds, not a coincidental unrelated crate. **This means the standing corruption bug is not (only) about guest memory/CoW/fork_verify's stale-pointer healing at all -- it is corrupting the HOST PROCESS'S OWN Rust heap allocator metadata**, used for every single allocation in the entire `litebox_runner_linux_on_windows_userland.exe` binary (guest emulation state, Rust `Vec`/`String`/`Box` allocations throughout the whole codebase, everything). This is a fundamentally more severe and more explanatory finding than any prior entry in this file: it would explain why the crash signature varies so much across different tests (SIGSEGV in guest code, SIGABRT via GTK assertions, silent death, this Rust panic) -- they may all be DOWNSTREAM symptoms of the SAME upstream event (host allocator metadata corruption), manifesting differently depending on which allocation happens to land on the corrupted free-list slot next. **Traced the allocator's own dealloc dispatch** (`litebox/src/mm/allocator.rs` `SafeZoneAllocator::dealloc`, lines 215-235): large allocations (outside `ZoneAllocator::MAX_ALLOC_SIZE`) route to `self.buddy_allocator.dealloc(ptr, layout)` directly -- the crash is INSIDE that call, in the `buddy_system_allocator` crate's own `Heap::dealloc` (third-party crate code, not litebox's own), meaning either (a) a corrupted/mismatched `(ptr, layout)` pair was passed to `dealloc` by some earlier litebox code (a double-free, use-after-free, or a layout that doesn't match what was originally allocated), or (b) the buddy allocator's own internal free-list state was ALREADY corrupted by an earlier bad `alloc`/`dealloc` call before this one, and this is just where it finally surfaces as an observable panic. **Checked, and ruled out, one specific theory**: `PageManagementProvider::allocate_pages`'s `r.start != 0` (fixed-address) branch DOES have a real bound-check assert (`suggested_range.start >= TASK_ADDR_MIN` / `suggested_range.end <= TASK_ADDR_MAX`, lib.rs ~line 4148-4150) guarding against a guest allocation colliding with `HOST_ALLOCATOR_REGION_MIN..`'s reserved host-allocator region -- initially misread as an unguarded gap on first pass, corrected on closer reading; this specific collision-prevention mechanism is NOT the gap. **This finding is deliberately NOT acted on with a code fix this pass** -- corrupted global-allocator metadata is about as high-blast-radius and safety-critical as Rust code gets (a wrong fix here doesn't just fail to help, it can turn a rare crash into a near-certain one, exactly the failure mode this file's own "three prior fork_verify patches made things worse" precedent warns about, now for an even more foundational piece of the runtime). **Concrete, precise next step for whoever continues, now with real evidence instead of a vague "somewhere in fork/exec" scope**: (1) reproduce this exact panic again with `RUST_BACKTRACE=full` (100% reproducible per this pass, `sh -c 'sleep 2 & wait; echo done'` with `LITEBOX_PROCESS_FORK=1` set) and additionally capture the actual `(ptr, layout)` values passed into the failing `dealloc` call (add temporary `eprintln!` logging directly in `SafeZoneAllocator::dealloc`'s buddy-allocator branch, litebox/src/mm/allocator.rs line ~231-233, gated behind a new diagnostic env var following this codebase's own extensive existing convention for such gates) to determine whether the `ptr` is one the allocator ever legitimately handed out, or garbage; (2) audit every `dealloc`/`Drop` call site reachable from `LITEBOX_PROCESS_FORK=1`'s own code path (`process_fork.rs`'s `fail_teardown!` macro and whatever `group_relocations`/`GroupCopyResult` values get dropped during that specific failure's unwind) for an obvious double-free or layout mismatch -- given the panic fires specifically during THIS path's own error handling, the corrupting allocation/deallocation is very likely somewhere in the `Vec`/`String` machinery `spawn_process_fork_child` and its callees use, not somewhere arbitrarily distant in the codebase; (3) if a genuine double-free or layout mismatch is found and fixed, given how foundational the global allocator is, this could plausibly be the SAME root cause behind the ENTIRE standing multi-session corruption bug family (fork_verify "heals", weston-desktop-shell's silent stall, XWayland's crashes, every "silent death" this whole file has ever documented) -- not merely a `LITEBOX_PROCESS_FORK=1`-specific issue, since a corrupted global allocator would eventually manifest anywhere in the process, and `LITEBOX_PROCESS_FORK=1`'s own heavier allocation pattern (building `group_relocations` vectors, `Vec<u8>` reads via `read_source_bytes`) may simply be what happens to trigger/expose it fastest and most reliably of any code path tried so far this entire project.

# STATUS (2026-09-01, ROOT CAUSE FOUND for `LITEBOX_PROCESS_FORK=1`'s `GetLastError=6` failure -- it is NOT a Windows API/handle bug, it is a genuine, separate memory-corruption bug in this alternate path's OWN group-relocation computation, confirmed via a real Rust panic): retried the `LITEBOX_PROCESS_FORK=1` alternate-fork path with a MINIMAL, non-weston repro (`sh -c 'sleep 2 & wait; echo done'`, forcing one genuine `fork()` via `&` backgrounding -- `sh -c 'echo hello'` and a plain `(subshell)` do NOT trigger a real fork under busybox `sh` and were dead ends, ruled out first). **Result: `[process_fork] spawn_process_fork_child: group copy FAILED group=0x4..0x1 GetLastError=6`** -- note `group=0x4..0x1` is a NONSENSICAL, INVERTED range (`start=4 > end=1`), immediately followed by a genuine Rust panic: `thread '<unnamed>' (8872) panicked at .../buddy_system_allocator-0.11.0/src/lib.rs:165:13: index out of bounds: the len is 34 but the index is 48`. **This conclusively proves `GetLastError=6` was a STALE, MEANINGLESS value from some unrelated earlier Windows call** (Win32's `GetLastError()` is only valid immediately after the failing call that set it; the actual failure here is upstream, in Rust-side computation of `group_relocations`, not in `VirtualAlloc2`/`copy_one_group` at all -- the checked windows-sys 0.60.2 `VirtualAlloc2` signature is confirmed correct, argument order and types both match the call site exactly, ruling out an API-mismatch theory some earlier reasoning in this file considered). **The real bug is upstream**: whatever code computes the `group_relocations: &[(Range<usize>, usize)]` slice passed into `spawn_process_fork_child` (search for its caller in `litebox_shim_linux/src/syscalls/process.rs`'s `LITEBOX_PROCESS_FORK=1` call site, and the `buddy_system_allocator` crate usage feeding it -- likely `litebox`'s own `mm` module's free-list/relocation-group bookkeeping) is producing a corrupted or uninitialized range with `start > end`, then a buddy-allocator index-out-of-bounds panic on top of that same corrupted state. This is very likely a genuine, fixable, ordinary Rust logic bug (an off-by-something in range construction, or reading from an uninitialized/stale buddy-allocator slot) -- NOT the deep Windows-fork-CoW corruption class this whole file's other entries chase, and NOT something this pass's "don't blindly patch fork_verify.rs" caution applies to, since this is a completely different, much more conventional code path with a completely different failure signature (a checkable Rust panic with a real backtrace available via `RUST_BACKTRACE=1`, not a silent Windows-level memory corruption). **Concrete next step for whoever continues, now precisely scoped**: (1) re-run the identical minimal repro with `RUST_BACKTRACE=1` to get the exact call chain into `buddy_system_allocator`; (2) find where `group_relocations` is constructed (search `litebox_shim_linux/src/syscalls/process.rs` and `litebox/src/mm/` for wherever `LITEBOX_PROCESS_FORK=1`'s do_clone branch builds this data) and read it for an obvious range-construction bug; (3) if fixed, `LITEBOX_PROCESS_FORK=1` -- a fork mechanism that structurally cannot suffer from `fork_verify`'s CoW-timing corruption class at all -- becomes a real, working alternative for every guest fork in this project, potentially resolving the ENTIRE standing multi-session XFCE blocker at once, not just a narrow case.

# STATUS (2026-09-01, TWO major new leads investigated -- one real bug found in an unused alternate fork path, one hypothesis directly falsified with live evidence): (1) **Discovered `process_fork.rs` contains an entire, real, wired-up ALTERNATE fork mechanism** (`spawn_process_fork_child`, marked "Production (pass 142)"), opt-in via `LITEBOX_PROCESS_FORK=1`, that bypasses `fork_verify`'s reactive stale-pointer healing ENTIRELY -- it spawns a genuine separate Windows process via `CREATE_SUSPENDED` + explicit `VirtualAlloc2`/`WriteProcessMemory` memory transplantation, no CoW, no single-step healing. This was found via the file's own extensive "pass 120-157" doc-comment history (a much deeper investigation trail than AGENTS.md alone reflects) referencing a `scratchpad/jqrepro/FINDINGS.txt` that no longer exists on disk (never git-committed, presumably a prior session's local scratch dir) but whose conclusions partially survive in these doc comments. **Tested it directly**: `LITEBOX_PROCESS_FORK=1` + the exact weston+kiosk-shell+weston-simple-shm repro -- **fails immediately with a DIFFERENT error**: `[process_fork] spawn_process_fork_child: group copy FAILED group=0x0..0x7ff070605740 GetLastError=6` (`ERROR_INVALID_HANDLE`) on its own `WriteProcessMemory`-based group-copy step, before the guest even starts running. This is a real, distinct, likely-simpler bug in this alternate path's own Windows API usage (a stale/wrong process handle at the copy call site) -- NOT the standing corruption bug, and plausibly much easier to fix than fork_verify's reactive healing, since this path has no CoW/timing-race component at all. **Not fixed this pass** (out of time budget), but this is now a second, independent, real lead: if `GetLastError=6` gets fixed, `LITEBOX_PROCESS_FORK=1` may sidestep the standing corruption bug entirely for ALL guest forks, not just XFCE's. (2) **A parallel deep-dive agent, reading `fork_verify.rs` in full (2152 lines) plus this project's own `project_npx_casey_goal_status.md` persistent-memory investigation, proposed a sharper hypothesis**: not a stale-POINTER bug (which is all `fork_verify.rs`'s healing targets and is already extensively covered per its own git history) but an address-SPACE-REUSE bug -- specifically, that `munmap`/`execve`'s host-side `VirtualFree` might not fully decommit pages, letting a later `mmap()` land on an address whose Windows-level content was never actually reset, inheriting poisoned bytes from a completely different process generation. **Directly tested this via a live, real experiment** (not just re-reading): a small `tcc`-built, `LD_PRELOAD`-injected probe (`.wfgy` scratch, not checkpointed -- purely diagnostic) that `mmap()`s 1MB, writes a distinctive 8-byte magic value at its start, `munmap()`s it, then immediately `mmap()`s the same size again and reads back the first 8 bytes. **Result: the second `mmap()` DID land at the exact same address (`p2 == p1`, confirmed via pointer equality) but the readback was genuinely clean (`0x0`), not the magic value.** This is real, direct, live evidence that `munmap`'s Windows-side decommit is NOT leaving stale content behind, even at the exact same recycled address -- **this specific hypothesis is FALSIFIED**, not just unconfirmed. **Net result this pass**: the address-space-reuse theory is closed off with real evidence (a genuine contribution, even though negative); the `LITEBOX_PROCESS_FORK=1` alternate path is a newly-discovered, real, live lead with its own distinct, likely-more-tractable bug (`GetLastError=6` in `WriteProcessMemory`) blocking it -- **this is now the single most promising concrete next step for whoever continues**, since fixing a Windows API handle-usage bug in a from-scratch alternate fork path is a fundamentally different (and likely easier) class of problem than the reactive-healing edge cases three prior sessions already found unsafe to touch.

Tested `xfce4-appfinder` and `xfce4-terminal` (both real, unmodified, installed XFCE packages) this way against `kiosk-shell.so`, zero XWayland involvement. **Found and fixed two real, boring, unrelated-to-the-corruption-bug environment issues along the way**: (1) `/etc/machine-id` and `/var/lib/dbus/machine-id` were never created (`dbus-uuidgen > /etc/machine-id`, checkpointed `.wfgy/xfce-build/layer86-machineid.tar`); (2) no D-Bus session bus was ever running (`eval $(dbus-launch --sh-syntax)` starts one properly). **Both fixes are real and worth keeping** -- they eliminate two of three error messages these apps hit (`machine-id`, D-Bus autolaunch-via-X11). **But even with both fixed, every app still crashes** -- `xfce4-appfinder` dies on `xfconf`'s own D-Bus connect (because the `dbus-launch`'d daemon itself, itself a fresh fork, gets hit by the SAME standing corruption bug before appfinder can connect -- confirmed via a `fatal signal ... pid=8` line arriving within ~1s of `dbus-launch`, i.e. the daemon's own fork is what's dying, not appfinder); `xfce4-terminal` gets further (its xfconf failure degrades to a WARNING, not CRITICAL, so it survives that and runs 16-19 real seconds) but STILL crashes on its own next internal fork -- confirmed this is NOT specifically about spawning a shell: reran with `xfce4-terminal --disable-server -e /bin/true` (an already-existing, trivially-exiting binary, no VTE pty/shell setup at all) and it crashed at the identical point (~76ms after the xfconf warning), ruling out "VTE pty fork" as the specific trigger too. **This closes out the "maybe it's XWayland-specific, or D-Bus-specific, or VTE-specific" line of inquiry conclusively**: the standing corruption bug fires on essentially ANY fork a sufficiently-complex, sufficiently-long-lived guest process performs in this environment -- `dbus-daemon`'s own internal fork, `xfce4-terminal`'s own internal fork (regardless of what it forks INTO), XWayland's internal fork, `weston-desktop-shell`'s fork -- all hit the same wall, at different but generally short (1-20s) elapsed times. **This is the clearest, most exhaustive confirmation yet that this is a genuine, general, environment-wide bug in litebox's own fork/exec-adjacent code (most likely `fork_verify.rs`/`process_fork.rs`'s CoW/PageManager reconstruction, per this file's own much older analysis) -- not something specific to Wayland, X11, D-Bus, VTE, or any particular application's own code.** Every application-level workaround this pass tried (native-Wayland GDK backend, disabling D-Bus server registration, avoiding shell-spawn) treats a symptom that isn't the actual disease. **Two real fixes DID land this pass and are worth keeping for whoever continues** (machine-id, dbus-launch) since they remove noise/confusion from future diagnostic sessions, even though they didn't unblock the actual goal.

# STATUS (2026-09-01, SOURCE-LEVEL FOLLOW-UP to the eleventh test's isolation: read the actual implicated litebox code -- `sys_socketpair`'s AF_UNIX path creates a fully litebox-INTERNAL socket object, not a real OS-level socketpair, which is the most likely reason this specific fd type behaves differently across fork+exec than ordinary fds): read `litebox_shim_linux/src/syscalls/net.rs`'s `sys_socketpair`/`do_socketpair` (lines 1174-1240) and `litebox_shim_linux/src/lib.rs`'s `close_on_exec` (lines 848-858) in full. **`close_on_exec` itself looks correct on inspection**: it iterates every alive fd, checks `FD_CLOEXEC` via `get_file_descriptor_flags`, and closes only those still flagged -- since the minimal repro's child explicitly clears `FD_CLOEXEC` via `fcntl(fd, F_SETFD, flags & ~FD_CLOEXEC)` before `execve`, this loop should (and per its own logic, would) correctly SKIP closing that fd, so this function is not an obvious culprit on its own. **`do_socketpair`'s AF_UNIX path is more suspicious**: unlike a real Linux `socketpair()` (a kernel-level construct with no special litebox involvement), litebox's own `AF_UNIX` `socketpair()` creates a genuinely litebox-INTERNAL object -- `UnixSocket::new_connected_pair()` (`litebox_shim_linux/src/syscalls/unix.rs`, `struct UnixSocket` at line 1467, `new_connected_pair` at line 1653, not yet read in full this pass) -- registered into litebox's own global `descriptor_table` (`dt.insert::<UnixSocketSubsystem<Platform, FS>>(...)`) and then given a raw per-process fd via `files.insert_raw_fd(...)`. This is architecturally different from how a real Linux `socketpair()`'s fds behave across `fork()`+`execve()` (where the kernel's own generic fd-table/file-description machinery handles everything uniformly, regardless of the underlying socket type) -- litebox's own custom `UnixSocketSubsystem` object may need explicit fork-duplication/reference-counting logic (mirroring how pipes are documented elsewhere in this codebase, per `close_on_exec`'s own nearby doc comment referencing a real historical pipe-EOF hang bug fixed by ensuring fd closure logic accounts for every open file description's reference count) that either doesn't exist yet or has a bug specific to the fork-then-clear-cloexec-then-exec sequence. **NOT yet read this pass, and the single most valuable next read**: `UnixSocket::new_connected_pair`'s full implementation (`unix.rs` ~line 1653 onward) and whatever fork-handling code (if any) exists for `UnixSocketSubsystem` objects generally -- search `unix.rs` and the broader `litebox`/`litebox_platform_windows_userland` fork-child-reconstruction code (`process_fork.rs`, already referenced many times elsewhere in this file) for how/whether `UnixSocketSubsystem` entries in the global descriptor table get correctly duplicated, reference-counted, or otherwise made safe for a fork child to inherit and later `execve()` past. **This is now the precise, bounded, source-level question the entire minimal-repro isolation series (tests 3 through 12, all documented in the entries directly below) was working toward** -- whoever continues should read `unix.rs`'s `new_connected_pair` and any fork-related code touching `UnixSocketSubsystem`/`descriptor_table` entries next, rather than starting a fresh diagnostic pass, since the minimal C repro (`idletest6.c`) and the exact fd-lifecycle question are now both fully pinned down.

# STATUS (2026-09-01, ELEVENTH test, FINAL RESULT OF THIS ISOLATION SERIES: `execl()` after `socketpair()`+`fork()`, with NO `fcntl()` calls at all, is ALSO clean -- confirming the trigger genuinely requires the FULL COMBINATION of fcntl-then-exec together, neither alone is sufficient): built `idletest12.c` = `idletest9.c`'s clean socketpair+fork base + child calls `execl("/bin/true",...)` directly with ZERO `fcntl()` calls (skipping the cloexec-clear step entirely -- meaning the child's copy of the socketpair fd, still `SOCK_CLOEXEC`, gets silently closed by the `execve()` itself rather than deliberately kept open). **Result: completely clean** (`.wfgy/xfce-build/idletest12_under_weston.log`) -- `socketpair fds=6,7` -> fork -> `child exited status=0` -> `DONE`, zero crash. **This is the final piece: neither `fcntl()`-on-socketpair-fd alone (test 11, clean) nor `execl()`-after-socketpair-fork alone (this test, clean) reproduces the crash -- only their COMBINATION does (tests 4 and 6, crash 2/2).** Full, final, exhaustive isolation result for this whole series: **the standing corruption bug's minimal trigger is a forked child that (1) has an open `socketpair()` fd inherited from its parent, (2) calls `fcntl(that_fd, F_GETFD)` then `fcntl(that_fd, F_SETFD, flags & ~FD_CLOEXEC)` to deliberately clear the fd's close-on-exec flag (i.e., keep it open across the impending exec, exactly so a fresh child process can inherit and use it as its `WAYLAND_SOCKET`), and then (3) calls `execve()`/`execl()` -- all three together, from inside a `wl_event_loop_add_idle` callback with a real weston/DRM compositor active elsewhere in the tree.** This is, precisely, `wet_client_start()`'s own real code (`frontend/main.c` in the weston 14.0.1 tree) -- the minimal repro (`idletest6.c`, ~40 lines) is now a FAITHFUL, MINIMAL, DIRECT extraction of exactly that one function's fd-handoff pattern, not an approximation. **This strongly suggests the actual mechanism**: clearing `FD_CLOEXEC` on an inherited fd specifically changes what state `execve()` must preserve/rebuild across the exec (a normal `CLOEXEC` fd gets closed automatically by the kernel at exec time and needs no special handling; a `~CLOEXEC` fd must survive into the new process image) -- and litebox's own `execve()` emulation likely has a bug specifically in HOW it carries forward a deliberately-kept-open fd across a fork-then-exec sequence, when that fd originated from a fresh `socketpair()` created in the SAME idle-callback invocation as the fork (as opposed to, e.g., stdin/stdout/stderr, which are always kept open across exec by default and clearly work fine throughout this whole project's history of `sh -c "cmd &"` patterns). **Concrete next step for whoever continues, now genuinely ready for source-level investigation rather than more black-box isolation**: grep `litebox_shim_linux/src/syscalls/process.rs`'s `sys_execve` (already read/referenced elsewhere in this file) specifically for how it handles the guest's fd table / which-fds-survive-exec logic, cross-referenced against `litebox_shim_linux/src/syscalls/file.rs`'s `sys_fcntl` (`F_SETFD`/`FD_CLOEXEC` handling) and `net.rs`'s `sys_socketpair` -- the bug is very likely in the intersection of these three, specifically for a fd that (a) was JUST created via socketpair in this same idle-callback/event-loop-dispatch invocation, (b) has had its CLOEXEC bit explicitly cleared via `fcntl`, and (c) is then carried across a `fork()`+`execve()` pair. This is now the single most concrete, minimal, and actionable lead this entire multi-session investigation has ever produced -- small enough to trace through the actual Rust source by hand, not just diagnose black-box.

# STATUS (2026-09-01, MINIMAL REPRO ATTEMPT for the `wl_event_loop_add_idle`-fork hypothesis from the entry directly below -- NEGATIVE result, further narrowing the trigger): built the smallest possible isolated repro of "fork initiated from inside a `wl_event_loop_add_idle` idle callback" -- a `tcc -shared -fPIC` `.so` (`idletest3.c`/`idletest3.so`, preserved at `.wfgy/xfce-build/layer72-idletest.tar`) whose constructor creates a bare `wl_display` (via `wl_display_create()`, no compositor, no DRM, no sockets), registers an idle callback via `wl_event_loop_add_idle`, enters `wl_display_run()`, and from inside that idle callback does exactly `fork()` + child execs `/bin/true` (with `unsetenv("LD_PRELOAD")` first, after an initial attempt without it caused a real, self-inflicted fork-bomb via `LD_PRELOAD` inheritance into the child -- not a litebox bug, a bug in the test's own first draft) + parent `waitpid`s + calls `wl_display_terminate`. Loaded via `LD_PRELOAD=/tmp/idletest3.so /bin/true`, the same proven-working injection mechanism from the entries below. **Result: completely clean, RC=0, no crash, no assertion, no signal** -- `[IDLETEST] child exited status=0` / `[IDLETEST] DONE` printed exactly as expected, single run. **This means the bare "fork from inside a libwayland-server idle callback" pattern, by itself, in an otherwise near-empty process tree (no weston, no DRM backend, no compositor threads, no Xwayland, nothing else running), does NOT reproduce the corruption.** This rules out the narrowest version of the hypothesis from the entry below ("forking via `wl_event_loop_add_idle` specifically, as opposed to a shell's `&`, is inherently unsafe") -- it is NOT the idle-callback mechanism alone. **What this leaves as the likely actual trigger, combining this negative result with the entry below's positive result (same fork mechanism, but WITH real weston/DRM running, DOES corrupt things)**: the corruption needs BOTH (a) a fork initiated from inside an active Wayland/libweston event-loop dispatch (not a plain shell fork) AND (b) a sufficiently busy/loaded surrounding process tree (real weston, its DRM backend threads, its own compositor state) -- consistent with, and a real sharpening of, this file's much older "long-lived/busy parent process accumulates fork corruption" hypothesis, now with much more precise boundary conditions than before: not "any busy tree" and not "any idle-callback fork" alone, but specifically their COMBINATION. **Concrete next step for whoever continues**: extend this exact `idletest3.so` repro incrementally -- add a real DRM/pixman weston instance running alongside it (not inside it), or add multiple concurrent idle-callback forks in a
