`LD_PRELOAD` constructor injection) from this session's own hard-won discoveries.

# STATUS (2026-09-01, FOLLOW-UP, further narrows the unifying finding immediately below: it is NOT `weston-desktop-shell`'s own CODE that corrupts the environment -- it is weston's own internal FORK MECHANISM firing via `wl_event_loop_add_idle(loop, launch_desktop_shell_process, shell)` that does, independent of what binary gets launched): tested the obvious next lever from the entry below -- weston.ini's `[shell] client=<path>` option (read by `shell_configuration()` in `desktop-shell/shell.c` line ~529, defaulting to the real `weston-desktop-shell` binary) lets you override WHAT `desktop-shell.so` launches as its client, without touching whether it launches one at all. Set `client=/bin/true` (checkpointed `.wfgy/xfce-build/layer71-noclient.tar`) so weston's own auto-launch spawns a totally inert, instantly-exiting binary instead of the real desktop-shell client. **Result: `weston-simple-shm` STILL crashes identically** (`Assertion failed: display->display (../clients/simple-shm.c: create_display: 585)` -> SIGABRT, `.wfgy/xfce-build/noclient_test.log` line 454-456) -- byte-for-byte the same failure as with the REAL `weston-desktop-shell` client. **This rules out `weston-desktop-shell`'s own code/logic as the corruption source entirely** -- `/bin/true` has no meaningful code path, no Wayland calls, nothing in common with the real client except that weston forked+exec'd it via the exact same `wet_client_start()`/idle-callback mechanism `desktop-shell.so`'s `wet_shell_init()` wires up unconditionally the moment that shell module loads (`wl_event_loop_add_idle(loop, launch_desktop_shell_process, shell)`, shell.c line ~4996). **This means the actual trigger is narrower and more precise than "any fork corrupts things" (which would contradict this file's own extensive prior evidence of MANY safe `&`-backgrounded forks in shell scripts throughout this whole project) -- it is specifically THIS fork mechanism**: a fork initiated from INSIDE weston's own Wayland event loop via `wl_event_loop_add_idle`, as opposed to a shell's ordinary `fork()`+`execve()` for a background job. This is a genuinely new, sharper, more falsifiable distinction than anything in this file's prior entries about the standing fork_verify bug, which mostly discuss forks generically without distinguishing HOW the fork was initiated. **Concrete next step for whoever continues**: check whether `kiosk-shell.so` or other weston shell modules ALSO use `wl_event_loop_add_idle` for their own optional client-launch features (if any), to see whether this specific idle-callback-fork pattern is reproducible outside `desktop-shell.so` too -- if so, that's the real, minimal, isolated repro case this whole investigation has been missing: something specific to forking from inside libweston's own event loop, not desktop-shell's code, not weston-desktop-shell's code, and not "any fork" generically.

# STATUS (2026-09-01, UNIFYING FINDING: `desktop-shell.so`'s own fork of `weston-desktop-shell` corrupts the environment for ANY SUBSEQUENT Wayland client, not just itself -- this explains BOTH agents' "any late-launched client's libwayland state looks broken" observations from the two entries immediately below as artifacts of the SAME single root cause, not two separate mysteries): re-ran the exact `weston-simple-shm` client that was already proven, earlier this same day, to render live/animated content perfectly under `--shell=kiosk-shell.so` (zero crashes, `.wfgy/xfce-build/simpleshm_screenshot.png`) -- but this time launched it against a weston instance running `--shell=desktop-shell.so` (the REAL XFCE-oriented shell) instead. **Result: `weston-simple-shm` itself now crashes**, with a clean, unambiguous, non-corrupted assertion message (not a silent death): `Assertion failed: display->display (../clients/simple-shm.c: create_display: 585)` followed by `fatal signal: terminating task signal=Signal(6) [SIGABRT]` (`.wfgy/xfce-build/simpleshm_under_dshell.log` line 458). This means `wl_display_connect(NULL)` itself returned NULL for a SECOND, INDEPENDENT, real-distro-compiled client (not desktop-shell's own bundled client, not a hand-rolled tcc diagnostic) -- **directly explaining the two entries immediately below this one**, where agent-built diagnostic tooling (`dsdiag.so` via tcc+LD_PRELOAD) and a claimed "distro weston-info" both saw `wl_proxy_get_version()` return 0 / a "page-aligned, looks-uninitialized" `wl_display` pointer and concluded "any late client's libwayland state is broken" -- **that framing was too broad and pointed at the wrong layer**: it is not "lateness" (re-confirmed by launching the tcc diagnostic at t=1s, the earliest possible point, in one of those entries -- still broken) and it is not general litebox/libwayland brokenness (directly disproven: the identical `weston-simple-shm` binary, launched the identical way, works PERFECTLY under `kiosk-shell.so` and fails ONLY under `desktop-shell.so`). **Confirmed via a clean control run**: `weston-simple-shm` under `kiosk-shell.so` (no `desktop-shell.so`, hence no `weston-desktop-shell` fork ever happens) succeeds again, cleanly, zero crash (`.wfgy/xfce-build/kiosk_sanity.log` line 443, `FIRST_CLIENT_DONE` with no assertion/signal lines anywhere in the log). **The single variable that flips a working client into a broken one is whether `desktop-shell.so` is the active shell module** -- i.e., whether `weston-desktop-shell`'s own `fork()`+`execve()` has happened in this compositor's lifetime. **New, sharper hypothesis, not yet proven but now well-motivated**: `weston-desktop-shell`'s own fork -- which per multiple entries in this file already independently triggers the standing `fork_verify`/mallocng corruption bug (see the STATUS entry below titled with "code=c0000005" / `rip=0x7ff7e06c3fd6`, captured via a genuinely different diagnostic path this same day) -- doesn't just corrupt or kill `weston-desktop-shell` ITSELF, but corrupts SHARED litebox-internal state (most plausibly something in the Unix-domain-socket-based Wayland transport's own shared bookkeeping, or a shared allocator region CoW-duplicated across the whole process tree) badly enough that it also breaks `wl_display_connect()` for every OTHER client that tries to connect to that SAME compositor afterward, even ones with no code-path relationship to desktop-shell whatsoever. This reframes the entire investigation: the real, single root cause blocking BOTH "why doesn't desktop-shell draw its own panel" AND "why can't ANY xfce client connect" may be the exact same standing fork corruption bug, manifesting through desktop-shell's fork specifically (since it's the one weston forks automatically, unconditionally, the moment `shell=desktop-shell.so` is configured) -- not two separate bugs needing two separate investigations. **Concrete next step for whoever continues**: test whether AVOIDING desktop-shell's automatic fork entirely (e.g., a weston shell config/flag that loads `desktop-shell.so` for its shell-protocol-server functionality WITHOUT auto-launching its bundled client -- check `wet_shell_init`'s `wl_event_loop_add_idle(loop, launch_desktop_shell_process, shell)` call in `desktop-shell/shell.c` for whether this is conditional on any config option not yet tried) restores clean multi-client connectivity even with `desktop-shell.so` loaded -- if so, the path forward is either (a) finding a way to suppress the auto-launch and instead launch a hand-controlled, carefully-isolated instance of `weston-desktop-shell` at a "safer" point in the process tree (early, before other forks, matching this file's own "checkpoint to a layer, don't compile+launch weston in the same invocation" finding from the entry below), or (b) treating this as unavoidable confirmation that fixing the standing fork_verify bug itself (not routing around it) is the only real path to a working XFCE desktop.

# STATUS (2026-09-01, desktop-shell `output_init()` instrumentation attempt: FIRST-EVER custom diagnostic code successfully COMPILED AND EXECUTED inside the guest against a LIVE weston -- but the three target questions remain UNANSWERED, blocked by a newly-isolated, precisely-located crash in `wl_proxy_marshal_array_flags`; TWO REAL, REUSABLE, PREVIOUSLY-UNDOCUMENTED FACTS ESTABLISHED, and one of this file's own prior claims FALSIFIED): the goal this pass was the single most valuable open question in this file -- get `fprintf` diagnostics into (1) `output_init()`'s entry, (2) `create_output()`'s entry printing whether `desktop->shell` is NULL, and (3) `main()`'s catch-up loop printing `desktop.shell`/`desktop.outputs` state -- and run them against a live compositor. **What was built and confirmed working**: fetched the real `clients/desktop-shell.c` (1612 lines) and `clients/window.c` (7336 lines) from the GitHub mirror (`gh api repos/wayland-mirror/weston/contents/<path>?ref=14.0.1` -- note `14.0.2` is a **404** on that mirror even though the guest ships weston **14.0.2**, so `14.0.1` is the only fetchable tag; the relevant functions are unchanged between them). A full desktop-shell.c rebuild was correctly judged infeasible (it needs `window.c` plus the whole cairo/pango toolkit), so instead a **structurally faithful replica** (`dsdiag`) was written: it reproduces `window.c`'s `display_create()` (registry + TWO roundtrips with `global_handler` still NULL, `wl_list_insert(d->global_list.prev, ...)` tail-append), `display_set_global_handler()`'s in-order replay, `desktop-shell.c`'s `global_handler`/`create_output`/`output_init`, and `main()`'s catch-up loop -- the same control flow, with `[DIAG]` prints at all three target points plus full `wl_list` next/prev/length dumps at every insert and iteration. Toolchain installed cleanly into the guest with `apk add --no-cache --no-scripts tcc musl-dev wayland-dev` (layer `.wfgy/xfce-build/layer40-build.tar`; note `xfce-layer30-westonclients.tar` does **NOT** actually contain tcc/wayland-dev despite this file implying otherwise -- it has neither `tcc`, `gcc`, `cc`, `meson`, `ninja`, nor `wayland-scanner`); `wayland-scanner client-header`/`private-code` on the fetched `protocol/weston-desktop-shell.xml` generated the protocol bindings, and `tcc -B/diag -I/diag ...` compiled everything with rc=0. Also confirmed the guest's live `/etc/xdg/weston/weston.ini` is `[core] shell=desktop-shell.so` / `[shell] background-color=0xff002244, panel-position=top, locking=false` -- so `want_panel=1`, meaning `main()`'s `weston_desktop_shell_set_panel_position(desktop.shell, ...)` on the line immediately before the catch-up loop genuinely would fault if `desktop.shell` were NULL, re-confirming this file's earlier "shell is provably non-NULL there" reasoning against the ACTUAL config in use.

**FALSIFIED, and this matters for every future diagnostic attempt in this environment: this file's own claim that "`tcc` never goes anywhere near the `ET_EXEC`/DLL-collision address range `cc1` does" is WRONG.** `tcc` 0.9.28rc (the Alpine package) produces **ET_EXEC** executables at a fixed low load address (confirmed by hand-reading the ELF header of a tcc-built binary in the guest: `e_type = 02 00` = ET_EXEC, `e_entry = 0x00603240`), and **it has no `-pie` support whatsoever** (`tcc: error: invalid option -- '-pie'`, and `-Wl,-pie` gives `unsupported linker option '-pie'` -- both confirmed live in the guest). **Any tcc-produced EXECUTABLE instantly kills the entire litebox runner process on `execve`, with zero output and zero crash markers** -- verified with a trivial `int main(){return 0;}`, which killed the whole runner (`EXIT=127`, the *shell itself* died, the very next `echo` in the script never ran) even with **no weston running at all and an otherwise idle process tree**. This is NOT the standing "late execve into a busy fork-heavy tree" bug -- it is a separate, deterministic, 100%-reproducible ET_EXEC-load failure, and it fully explains why this whole class of workaround has never worked. **The working alternative, confirmed live**: build the diagnostic as a `-shared -fPIC` **`.so`** (ET_DYN, which tcc does produce correctly) and inject it via `LD_PRELOAD` onto an ordinary distro binary (`LD_PRELOAD=/diag/dsdiag.so /bin/busybox true`), with all the work in an `__attribute__((constructor))`. **This works and is the first time in this project's documented history that custom, purpose-written diagnostic code has actually run inside the guest against a live weston compositor** -- confirmed by real captured output: `[DIAG] === dsdiag START (pid=12) ===`, `[DIAG] main: wl_list_init(&desktop.outputs) done. &desktop=0x1759fc78 &desktop.outputs=0x1759fc88 next=0x1759fc88 prev=0x1759fc88 length=0`, `[DIAG] connected. wl_display=0x161c0000`. Note this is NOT the `LD_PRELOAD` *interposition* that this file already documents as broken across 5 variants -- no symbol is being overridden here, the constructor merely runs its own code, exactly the "plain constructor-only shim DOES work" case this file already flagged as viable.

**Where it stops, isolated to a single function call by successive bisection**: the replica reaches `wl_display_get_registry()` and takes **SIGSEGV (exit 139)** there, every time, across 5 independent runs. Successive instrumentation narrowed it precisely. All libwayland interface data symbols resolve **correctly** -- `[DIAG] iface syms: &wl_registry_interface=0x161aca20 name='wl_registry' &wl_output_interface=0x161ac7a0 name='wl_output' &wl_compositor_interface=0x161ab780 &wl_shm_interface=0x161ab820 &weston_desktop_shell_interface=0x17bae8b0` -- so this is NOT a bad relocation of external data (the `.name` strings read back correctly as `'wl_registry'`/`'wl_output'`, proving the pointers are genuinely valid). Then three targeted probes: `[DIAG] probe A ok: fd=3` (`wl_display_get_fd`, an external libwayland call -- **works**), `[DIAG] probe B ok: version=0` (`wl_proxy_get_version`, another external libwayland call -- **returns without crashing**), `[DIAG] probe C: about to call wl_proxy_marshal_array_flags directly` -> **SIGSEGV, no further output**. **So the crash is specifically at `wl_proxy_marshal_array_flags`, and external libwayland calls in general are fine.** Two hypotheses were tested and BOTH ELIMINATED: (a) tcc's variadic-call ABI (this file already documents tcc varargs problems, and `wl_display_get_registry` is a static-inline wrapper around the variadic `wl_proxy_marshal_flags`) -- eliminated by rewriting every request through `wl_proxy_marshal_array_flags`, which is **fully non-variadic** (it takes a pre-built `union wl_argument[]`, exactly what libwayland's own variadic entry point builds internally) and takes only 6 arguments, all register-passed on x86-64 SysV with no stack args and no `%al` vector-count involved -- **the crash is byte-identical**; (b) constructor-ordering (my preloaded `.so`'s constructor running before libwayland's own init) -- eliminated by switching to `__attribute__((destructor))`, which runs at process exit after all initialization is complete -- **also byte-identical**. The symbol genuinely exists in the guest's `libwayland-client.so.0.25.0` (verified via `strings`, alongside `wl_proxy_marshal_flags`, `wl_proxy_marshal_array_constructor`, etc.). **The one concrete unexplained anomaly, and the best lead for whoever continues**: `wl_proxy_get_version()` on the freshly-connected display returns **0**, where a real `wl_display` proxy should report **1** -- and `wl_display_connect()` returned the suspiciously round, page-aligned `0x161c0000` (stable across runs: `0x16200000`, `0x16240000`). A page-aligned low address is not what a `malloc`'d `wl_display` looks like; combined with `version=0`, this suggests **the `wl_display` struct libwayland handed back is not properly initialized in this environment** -- i.e. `wl_display_connect()` "succeeds" (non-NULL, and its `fd` field reads back correctly as 3) but its internal proxy/queue state is zeroed or otherwise wrong, so the first call that actually walks that internal state (`wl_proxy_marshal_array_flags`) faults. That is a **litebox-level `mmap`/allocator or shared-object-data issue, not a tcc codegen issue**, and it is directly checkable: the next session should print `wl_display_get_error()`/`errno` right after connect, dump the first ~64 bytes at the returned `wl_display*`, and compare that byte pattern against the same dump taken from inside a distro-built client, to confirm or kill the "zeroed/mis-mapped struct" reading.

**Corroborating control result (real, non-tcc client, SAME launch path)**: ran `weston-info` -- a genuine distro-built Wayland client, no tcc involved anywhere -- at exactly the same point in the same script against the same live weston. **It produced ZERO output lines** (not one `interface:` line), where a healthy run enumerates every global. This is important because it means the failure is **probably NOT tcc-specific at all** -- a real, ordinary, correctly-built Wayland client launched at this same point also fails to enumerate the registry. That is a fresh, independent line of evidence pointing at the same conclusion this file's other entries keep circling: something about a client `execve`'d into an already-running weston process tree leaves its libwayland connection state broken. Log: `.wfgy/xfce-build/real_client1.log` (the subsequent `weston-simple-shm` in that same script then stalled entirely, the familiar standing bug). **This also reframes the whole `output_init()` mystery**: if a plain `weston-info` cannot even enumerate globals when launched this way, then the real `weston-desktop-shell` -- which weston itself `execve`s into exactly this kind of already-busy tree -- may be failing for the same environmental reason, which would make "why doesn't the catch-up loop fire" the wrong question and "why is a late-launched client's libwayland connection state broken" the right one.

**Also observed, worth recording as an operational fact**: putting the `tcc` compile step and the weston launch in the SAME guest script reproducibly (3/3 runs: `dsdiag_run3.log`, `run4`, `run5`, all plateauing at the identical 79143-byte log size) kills the run right after weston's compositor-init burst, whereas doing the compile in a **separate, earlier runner invocation** and then running a launch-only script lets it get all the way to the client launch (5/5 runs). This is consistent with -- and is a small, cheap, reproducible repro for -- this file's own "long-lived parent process accumulates corruption from watching its own children's fork/exec cycles" hypothesis: the only difference between the two shapes is one extra heavy fork/exec (tcc) in the shell's history before weston starts. **Recommended practice for future passes: never compile and launch weston in the same guest invocation; always checkpoint the build to a layer first.**

**Honest bottom line**: the three target questions -- is `output_init()` ever called, is `desktop->shell` NULL in `create_output()`, and what do `desktop.shell`/`desktop.outputs` actually contain in the catch-up loop -- are **still unanswered**. But the blocker is now a *different, much narrower, and precisely located* one than at the start of this pass: not "we have no way to compile or run instrumentation" (that is now SOLVED and reusable -- tcc `.so` + `LD_PRELOAD` constructor, with the ET_EXEC trap documented so nobody loses another pass to it), but a single, specific, reproducible fault at `wl_proxy_marshal_array_flags` with one concrete anomalous data point (`wl_proxy_get_version()==0` on a freshly-connected display) to chase, plus independent corroboration from a real distro client that the problem is environmental rather than tcc-specific. Artifacts: source `dsdiag_so.c` and the built layers (`.wfgy/xfce-build/layer59.tar` = built `.so`, `layer65.tar` = destructor variant, `layer40-build.tar` = toolchain-only base); logs `dsdiag_run1/3/4/5/6/7/8/9/10.log`, `real_client1.log`, `probe2.log`, `probe3.log`, `buildso*.log`, all under `.wfgy/xfce-build/`.

# STATUS (2026-09-01, DIAGNOSTIC-ONLY pass: first-ever real crash signature captured for the XWayland fork_verify wall via `LITEBOX_DIAG_FATALDUMP=1` -- exact exception code/address/registers now on record, closing the "3 logs with no fataldump" gap this file's own instructions flagged; NO fix attempted, per explicit instruction): re-ran the exact repro this file's top status entry already used (`kiosk-shell.so` + `--xwayland` + `LIBSEAT_BACKEND=noop`, `alpine-pinned2.tar` + `xfce-layer30-westonclients.tar`), this time WITH `LITEBOX_DIAG_FATALDUMP=1` set from the start (the prior 3 confirmations, `xfce_final_test{,2,3}.log`, all ran WITHOUT it and only showed fork_verify heal activity followed by silence). Reproduced the exact same deterministic wall a 4th time, but this run captured the actual crash. **Full sequence**: `[22:01:04.663] created wm, root 575` (XWayland's own internal wm/root-window setup, line 46722 of `.wfgy/xfce-build/agent_fataldump_xwayland.log`) prints successfully; ~0.2s later (t=40.930s) a SECOND Windows host thread (VEH-tracked as `host_tid=ThreadId(18)`, distinct from `ThreadId(17)` which had just finished an almost byte-identical fork_verify heal sequence for the wm-setup work moments earlier) begins undergoing the SAME heal sequence against the SAME `rip`/`stale_value` pattern (`stale_value=312539328` in both cases) -- strongly suggesting this second thread is a **fresh fork of the SAME already-JIT-translated code region** XWayland's wm-setup thread just ran, i.e. XWayland forking again internally right after finishing wm/root setup (plausibly a second helper -- session/logging daemon or a GLX/render-node worker; `Xwayland glamor: GBM Wayland interfaces not available` / `Failed to initialize glamor, falling back to sw` appears earlier in the log at line 45766-45767, so this is likely NOT a glamor/GL thread, since glamor already failed and fell back to software well before this point). This second thread's heal sequence runs through the same stages (`stale CODE pointer detected` x6, `stale CODE pointer in indirect call/jmp target slot` x1 patching `stale_value=312539328` -> `translated=957348032`, then `stale DATA pointer read detected` -> `stale DATA pointer previously loaded from memory slot ... patching slot in place` at t=40.937762600s, `load_address=957824936` `stale_value=313027936` `translated=957836640`) -- then, on the VERY NEXT recorded event for that thread (~1ms later, no further heal warning in between), the process crashes: **`[veh] RAWREGS tid=4d00 code=c0000005 addr=10405030 rip=7ff7e06c3fd6 rax=10405001 rbx=10401803 rcx=10405030 rdx=0 rsi=c4030 rdi=2a0 rsp=e78a80e110 rbp=e78a80e1a0`**, immediately followed by `[veh] RAWREGS tid=4d00 code=c000000d addr=0 rip=7ffb36712d2f rax=0 rbx=c0000001 ...` (STATUS_INVALID_PARAMETER, `rip` in ntdll's `0x7ffb...` range -- teardown/exception-dispatch noise, matching this file's own already-documented pattern for the second line of this exact two-line death signature). Process vanishes from `tasklist` immediately after (confirmed via live poll). **Correlation analysis**: (1) `code=c0000005` STATUS_ACCESS_VIOLATION's own faulting `rip=0x7ff7e06c3fd6` is in the LOW guest/JIT-translated code range (`0x7ff7e0...`, matching this session's own `translated_rip` values logged elsewhere, e.g. `957348032`=`0x390F...`-shaped, NOT `ntdll.dll`'s typical `0x7ffb...` range) -- this crash is happening in XWayland's OWN translated code, not inside ntdll's heap allocator this time (unlike the earlier `rip=0x7ffb3671587a` capture this file documents for the separate gcc/cc1 repro). (2) The faulting `addr=0x10405030` exactly equals `rcx`, and `rax=0x10405001` is `0x2f` (47 bytes) below it -- consistent with a struct-field dereference off a base pointer that is valid-but-stale (same small-negative/positive-offset-from-register shape as this file's own previously-documented `0x103c5030`/`rax=0x103c5001` capture from the `xfdesktop` fataldump run, though the concrete address differs -- same *pattern*, not a repeat of the identical byte address; register layout does NOT show an `[rdi-0x10]`-shaped access this time -- `rdi=0x2a0` here is small and unrelated to `addr`/`rcx`, so the previously-documented "group meta-slot"/`rdi-0x10` signature specifically was NOT reproduced in this capture; this looks like a different instruction/addressing mode hitting the same underlying stale-pointer corruption class). (3) A fork_verify DATA-pointer heal fires on the SAME thread roughly 1ms before the crash, patching a stale slot -- causally suspicious (the heal "fixes" one stale read but the crash could easily be the NEXT instruction's OWN separate stale dereference that the single-step healer hasn't caught up to yet, exactly the TOCTOU/lag hazard this file's own "gcc-hang root-cause sub-session" entry already root-caused in `fork_verify.rs`'s `end()`/`on_single_step` TF-flag handling, further below in this file) -- but this run's log has NO `clone: spawned new task` lines at all (that's a debug-level log line and this run used `LITEBOX_LOG=info`, not `debug`), so which specific fork produced the crashing thread (a genuine new XWayland-internal `fork()`, versus a plain new native Windows thread XWayland spawned via `pthread_create`/`clone(CLONE_THREAD)` for a worker) could NOT be disambiguated this pass -- a `LITEBOX_LOG=debug` (non-fataldump, faster) companion run to correlate `clone: spawned new task`/`tid=`/`host_tid=` against this exact timestamp window is the single most useful next step to sharpen this finding, not yet done this pass (time-boxed to the fataldump capture itself). **Net finding**: this is real, new, more precise evidence than existed before this pass (an actual exception code + faulting address + full register dump, not just "heals then silence") -- it does NOT change this file's own standing architectural diagnosis (the `fork_verify.rs` `end()`-doesn't-clear-`TF`-flag TOCTOU race, or an unhealed stale-pointer class the single-step healer's coverage gap misses) but gives it a second, independent, precisely-captured data point in the SAME shape as the earlier `0x103c5030` capture: a small-offset dereference off a plausibly-stale base pointer, in the guest's own translated code (not ntdll), on a thread that just underwent (or is a sibling of a thread that just underwent) heavy fork_verify healing. Raw log preserved at `.wfgy/xfce-build/agent_fataldump_xwayland.log` (47669 lines) for anyone who wants to re-examine it. **No fix attempted** -- per this task's own explicit constraint and this file's own standing "CLOSED DEAD END" instruction not to re-extend `fork_verify`'s step-bound coverage.

---

# STATUS (2026-08-31, XWayland re-confirmed, 3x reproducibly, to hit the standing CLOSED-DEAD-END fork_verify corruption bug deterministically -- not luck-based, not fixable by env/ordering tweaks): building directly on the entry below (which found `LIBSEAT_BACKEND=noop` + `kiosk-shell.so` + `weston-simple-shm` proves the core rendering pipeline is fully healthy), pushed further toward the real goal: launched weston with `--xwayland` from the very start of the process tree (minimum possible prior fork activity) under `LIBSEAT_BACKEND=noop`, and it DID succeed further than any prior attempt this whole session -- "xserver listening on display :0" AND (on retry) "created wm, root 575" (XWayland's own internal window-manager root-window creation, real, meaningful progress). But in **three separate, independent runs** (`.wfgy/xfce-build/xfce_final_test.log`, `xfce_final_test2.log`, `xfce_final_test3.log` -- one with a 30s flat sleep before the next command, one with a socket-readiness poll loop instead), the process reproducibly dies (vanishes from `tasklist`, zero Rust panic, silence after a burst of `fork_verify` heal activity) at almost the exact same point every time: immediately during/after XWayland's own internal fork for its window-manager/root-window setup, sometimes before `xfdesktop` even gets a chance to launch. This is NOT the earlier-hypothesized "non-deterministic, might get lucky on retry" pattern -- it is a **deterministic, reproducible wall** at this specific point in XWayland's own startup sequence, matching this file's own much-earlier "Current blocker (sub-session 23)" entry (search that heading below) to the letter: XWayland forks internally with no `--nofork`-equivalent flag, and this project's own prior investigation already tried and explicitly closed off three different attempts to extend `fork_verify`'s step-bound coverage to fix this class of bug, each attempt proven to cause WORSE, differently-shaped crashes (a false-positive heal against an expired map). **This pass did NOT re-attempt that closed fix** (per this file's own explicit instruction not to), and after 3 independent confirmations, further blind retries at this exact point are very unlikely to succeed by luck -- this is a structural, deterministic block, not a timing race. **Net honest status**: full "XFCE displaying completely normally" remains NOT achieved this pass, but the goalposts have moved substantially: (1) the core litebox+weston+Wayland rendering/client pipeline is now proven fully healthy (real client content renders, `weston-simple-shm` screenshot exists as proof), (2) `desktop-shell.so`'s specific `output_init` gap is isolated as a separate, narrower, still-unexplained bug, (3) XWayland's own internal fork is now confirmed, 3x reproducibly, to be an instance of the SAME standing, deep, previously-investigated-and-closed fork_verify corruption bug -- not a new mystery, but concrete, fresh, reproducible evidence for whoever eventually revisits that architecture with real debugger access. **The one thing that would move this forward that was NOT tried this pass**: building weston/XWayland from source with debug symbols and running under an actual native Windows debugger (WinDbg/x64dbg) attached to the litebox runner process, to catch the exact faulting instruction live rather than inferring from `fork_verify`'s own after-the-fact heal log -- every avenue available to a single scripted pass (env vars, launch ordering, retries, alternate shells, reduced fork count) has now been exhausted for this specific blocker.

# STATUS (2026-08-31, MAJOR NEW FINDING: isolated the black-screen bug to `desktop-shell.so` specifically -- proved the rest of the litebox+weston+Wayland rendering pipeline is fully healthy, and confirmed XWayland is blocked by the same standing, previously-closed fork_verify corruption bug, not a new issue): **Found `LIBSEAT_BACKEND=noop`** -- an env var libseat itself supports (confirmed via its own source-implied backend list: seatd, logind, noop) that makes weston skip `seatd`'s own fork entirely ("Seat opened with backend 'noop'" in the log) while still getting full DRM/root access (already running as uid 0). This eliminates one whole fork from the process tree for free, with zero downsides for a single-user sandboxed guest like this. **Definitive positive result**: ran weston with `--shell=kiosk-shell.so` (a simpler, non-panel weston-bundled shell) plus `LIBSEAT_BACKEND=noop`, then launched `weston-simple-shm` (from the newly-installed `weston-clients` package, `.wfgy/xfce-build/xfce-layer30-westonclients.tar`) as a real Wayland client -- **it rendered its full animated color-bars/concentric-circles test pattern, fullscreen, with ZERO crashes, and 20 real seconds of actual CPU scheduling** (`.wfgy/xfce-build/simpleshm_screenshot.png`) -- the first real, non-background-color CLIENT CONTENT this entire project has ever confirmed rendering. This conclusively proves the ENTIRE stack below the shell-client layer -- litebox's DRM/pixman rendering, Wayland transport, buffer sharing, compositor scene graph, and the guest's own execve/fork path for an ordinary well-behaved client -- is genuinely healthy and working. Also independently confirmed weston's own idle/no-client fallback state renders its configured `background-color` (weston.ini's `0xff002244`, a dark navy blue) correctly and stably under `kiosk-shell.so` (`.wfgy/xfce-build/kiosk_bluecheck.png`) -- real evidence the rendering pipeline handles arbitrary solid-color painting correctly too. **Negative result, isolating the actual remaining bug precisely**: the SAME `LIBSEAT_BACKEND=noop` env, with `--shell=desktop-shell.so` (weston's real XFCE-oriented shell) instead of kiosk-shell, STILL shows solid black after a full 90s stable window with zero crashes/respawns (`.wfgy/xfce-build/desktopshell_noop_check1.png`) -- this isolates the black-screen bug to `desktop-shell.so`'s/`weston-desktop-shell`'s own specific client logic (the previously-documented, still-unexplained "`output_init()` never runs" mystery -- see the many entries below on the registry-ordering race, the wire-trace proof `wl_compositor.create_surface` is never called, and the standing fork_verify-corrupts-post-exec-control-flow hypothesis) -- NOT a general litebox rendering/Wayland/fork-exec limitation, which is now proven healthy via the `weston-simple-shm` positive result above. **Also confirmed, independently, that XWayland is blocked by the SAME previously-documented, explicitly-CLOSED-DEAD-END fork_verify corruption bug** (see "Current blocker (sub-session 23)" further below in this file) -- ran the full `--xwayland` + kiosk-shell + `xfdesktop` script under the healthier `LIBSEAT_BACKEND=noop` environment and it STILL dies silently partway through (heavy `fork_verify` heal activity immediately preceding total silence, `.wfgy/xfce-build/kiosk_xfce_test1.log`), matching this bug's own well-documented signature exactly -- `LIBSEAT_BACKEND=noop` reduces but does NOT eliminate this specific corruption trigger, since XWayland's own internal fork (not seatd's) is the proximate cause here, and no `--nofork`-equivalent flag exists for Xwayland (confirmed, again, this pass). **Honest bottom line for whoever continues**: two independent, both-real blockers now stand between "current state" and "XFCE displaying completely normally" -- (1) `desktop-shell.so`'s own specific `output_init` control-flow gap (narrow, isolated, NOT a general litebox bug, but still unexplained after many sessions of investigation without live debugger access), and (2) XWayland's fork triggering the standing, explicitly-closed-dead-end fork_verify corruption bug (broad, deep, architectural, proven unsafe to patch blindly per three prior failed attempts). Fixing #1 alone (e.g., finding why desktop-shell's catch-up loop doesn't fire) would only yield a working weston PANEL/BACKGROUND, not full XFCE, since XFCE's actual desktop icons/GTK apps still need XWayland (#2) regardless. Both are real, both are now precisely scoped with concrete evidence, and neither was solved this pass -- but the search space for "what's still broken" has shrunk enormously: it is no longer "is anything in litebox's core stack broken" (no, proven working) but specifically these two named, separately-tracked issues.

# STATUS (2026-08-31, CORRECTION + one more real fix: the xkeyboard-config symlink-workaround fix below is CONFIRMED REQUIRED, not optional -- without it weston's compositor-side keymap compile fails FATALLY ("failed to compile global XKB keymap" -> "Failed to create a device" -> weston exits immediately, zero display ever attempted); the separate client-side `weston-desktop-shell` `XKB-822 "Failed to parse input xkb string"` error was WRONGLY characterized as "cosmetic" in the entry below -- re-testing found it puts `weston-desktop-shell` into a genuine crash-respawn loop ("disconnected, respawning..." 3x within ~60s in one run), which is the actual reason the panel/clock never draws in most runs, not a benign artifact; the one historical run that showed a live clock must have hit a lucky timing window where desktop-shell's OWN respawn happened to catch a working keymap compile, not evidence the error is harmless in general): **new fix**: `mkdir -p /etc/xkb` (xkbcommon's own error output explicitly names `/etc/xkb` as "1 include paths could not be added" -- it does not exist on this rootfs at all) -- checkpointed to `.wfgy/xfce-build/xfce-layer23-etcxkb.tar`. Verified across two back-to-back full runs: zero `weston-desktop-shell` respawns in either (versus 3 respawns in ~60s on the otherwise-identical layer WITHOUT `/etc/xkb`) -- this is real, reproducible improvement, not noise. **However**, the panel/clock still did not visibly render in either post-fix run's screenshot despite weston reaching its stable state with zero crashes/respawns and `Output 'Virtual-1' enabled` succeeding cleanly -- current best explanation is severe HOST CPU starvation this pass (`event0 ... client bug: event processing lagging behind by 65000-173000ms, your system is too slow` recurring across every run this pass, guest process CPU time observed frozen at 1-8 SECONDS total across 2-3+ MINUTES of real wall-clock time, i.e. roughly 2-5% actual scheduled CPU) rather than a further logic bug -- this host was simultaneously running many other heavy terminal/agent sessions during this pass (visible in the taskbar of the final screenshot: 5+ other active terminal windows plus Discord, Chrome, etc.), which is NOT representative of a normal/dedicated test run. **Next step for whoever continues**: re-run the identical repro (`xfce-layer23-etcxkb.tar` as `--resume-from`, the standard weston+seatd script from this file's own repro command) on a host with real CPU headroom (no other concurrent litebox instances or heavy agent sessions), and give it the full documented ~85-90s stable window under those less-starved conditions before judging the screenshot -- do not repeat this pass's mistake of running many concurrent litebox `--gui` instances (each ~1.5-1.8GB RAM) simultaneously, which both starves each individual instance's CPU time AND, per this file's own earlier entries, may be an actual contributing factor (not just a coincidence) to the standing corruption bug given its "long-lived/busy process tree" character. Also confirmed (again) `rm -f /run/seatd.sock` before `seatd -l debug &` remains necessary whenever resuming from ANY layer where seatd previously ran, including these new ones.

**FINAL UPDATE this pass**: extended the wait on the SAME already-running, already-stable `etcxkb_test1` process to over 6 minutes real wall-clock (well beyond the documented ~85-90s norm) rather than restarting -- confirmed via its own log that this was genuinely productive continued waiting, not a hang: `respawning` count stayed at 0 throughout (the crash-loop fix holds), and the guest's own internal clock drift grew from an initial small lag to "-205811ms" (205+ real seconds behind), proving the guest process is alive, scheduled occasionally, and simply never getting enough real CPU time to catch up -- not deadlocked. This is conclusive evidence the remaining blocker THIS SPECIFIC PASS is host CPU contention (this machine was concurrently running 5+ other heavy terminal/agent sessions the whole time, visible in every screenshot's taskbar), not a further code bug. **Both real fixes from this pass are validated and should be kept**: (1) copy `/usr/share/X11/xkb` to `/usr/share/xkeyboard-config-2` (do NOT symlink -- litebox's `in_mem.rs` `resolve_final_symlinks` does not follow intermediate-component directory symlinks, a separate real, general litebox filesystem-shim limitation, not fixed this pass), (2) `mkdir -p /etc/xkb`. Together these take weston from "fails to even start" (no fix) through "starts but desktop-shell crash-loops respawning forever" (fix 1 only) to "starts cleanly, desktop-shell does NOT crash-loop" (both fixes) -- each step is a real, verified improvement. What remains unverified, purely due to this pass's own host contention, is whether the panel/clock visibly renders once weston/desktop-shell get adequate real CPU time with both fixes applied -- this is the single concrete next thing to check, on a quieter host, before assuming any further code-level bug exists.

# STATUS (2026-08-31, two real bugs fixed this pass, but the standing late-execve mallocng/heap-corruption crash remains the blocker for full XFCE+XWayland; xfdesktop is itself a "late execve" victim): (1) **Fixed a genuine litebox filesystem-shim bug**: `xkeyboard-config` package's real files live under `/usr/share/X11/xkb/`, but its manifest also names an EMPTY `/usr/share/xkeyboard-config-2` directory that real Alpine normally makes a symlink to the former -- creating that symlink (`ln -s /usr/share/X11/xkb /usr/share/xkeyboard-config-2`) does NOT work in litebox: any multi-component path traversal THROUGH a directory-symlink fails with ENOTDIR (`ls symlink/child` -> "Not a directory"), reproduced even with a from-scratch same-mount (`/tmp`) symlink-to-directory, isolating it to `litebox/src/fs/in_mem.rs`'s `resolve_final_symlinks` (`in_mem.rs:198-231`), whose own doc comment states it deliberately ONLY resolves a FINAL path component's symlink, never an intermediate one -- this is a real, general, by-design gap (not a bug in the classic sense, but a functional gap versus real Linux/POSIX symlink semantics) affecting ANY directory symlink anywhere in the guest, not just xkeyboard-config. Worked around for this specific case by `cp -r`-ing the real directory tree instead of symlinking it (checkpointed to `.wfgy/xfce-build/xfce-layer21-xkbcopy.tar`) -- this cleanly eliminated the `XKB-338 "Couldn't find file rules/evdev"` / `XKB-595 "Cannot load XKB rules"` errors weston's OWN compositor-side keymap compile was hitting. (2) **Found and fixed a stale-lockfile bug**: `--export-writable-layer` preserves `/run/seatd.sock` across runs; a leftover non-socket file there makes `seatd` itself refuse to (re)bind on the next `--resume-from` of that layer ("Non-socket file found at socket path /run/seatd.sock, refusing to start"), cascading into weston's own "could not open seat" / "fatal: failed to create compositor backend" -- fix is `rm -f /run/seatd.sock` before `seatd -l debug &` in any script that resumes from a layer where seatd previously ran. **Important negative finding**: the separate, client-side `XKB-822 "Failed to parse input xkb string"` / "failed to compile keymap" error from `weston-desktop-shell` itself is CONFIRMED PRE-EXISTING and cosmetic/benign -- grepped every historical log this session touched (`identity_retest1.log` included, the SAME run that produced the first confirmed live, correctly-updating clock widget) and found this exact error present there too. It does not block panel/clock rendering and is not the real blocker; do not waste further time chasing it specifically. **The real, still-unresolved blocker**: with both fixes above applied (`xfce-layer21-xkbcopy.tar` as the resume-from base) and `LITEBOX_DIAG_FATALDUMP=1` enabled, the full XFCE script (weston+xwayland, then `xfdesktop -SEP` launched only after polling for a real `/tmp/.X11-unix/X0` socket) still dies silently partway through (`XFDESKTOP_LAUNCHED` never printed, process vanishes from `ps aux`/`tasklist` with zero Rust panic/fatal-signal log line) -- but this time, with fataldump's single-step trace enabled, the death is NOT fully silent: the log's last two lines before the process disappears are `code=c0000005` (`STATUS_ACCESS_VIOLATION`) at `addr=0x103c5030` (crashing `rip` in guest code, not litebox's own Rust code) immediately followed by `code=c000000d` (`STATUS_INVALID_PARAMETER`, likely teardown noise) -- this is the FIRST time this exact crash's real Windows exception code and faulting address have been captured (every previous session's attempts found only silent death with zero markers at INFO/DEBUG level, motivating `LITEBOX_DIAG_FATALDUMP=1` as the technique that finally surfaced it). The tiny delta between `rax=0x103c5001` and the faulting `addr=0x103c5030`/`rcx=0x103c5030` is consistent with a struct-field dereference off a corrupted/freed base pointer, matching this project's own long-standing mallocng `.meta=0`-style null/stale-pointer heap-corruption hypothesis -- `xfdesktop` here is itself exactly the "fresh client `execve`'d late in an already-busy, fork-heavy tree" shape this file's own earlier entries (see below) already isolated as the trigger class, so this is corroborating evidence for the existing hypothesis, not a new distinct bug. **Next step for whoever continues**: re-run the identical fataldump repro and use the newly-captured `rip`/faulting-`addr` values as a concrete starting point to map back to source (via the guest binary's own symbol table / the translated_rip fork_verify diagnostics already used elsewhere in this file), rather than starting the investigation from scratch again.

Built cleanly, ran the full XFCE `--gui` repro for 60+ real seconds: **zero crashes**, and a real Windows screenshot (`.wfgy/xfce-build/identity_screenshot1.png`) shows genuine `weston-desktop-shell` panel content -- an icon and a live clock widget reading "Mon Aug 31, 07:44 PM" -- rendered in the top area of the window. **Confirmed this is REAL, LIVE content, not a static artifact or a rendering glitch**: two more screenshots taken ~1 and ~3 minutes later show the SAME clock widget correctly advancing to "07:45 PM" then "07:47 PM", with the process remaining completely stable (zero crash markers) throughout the entire multi-minute observation window. This is the first time ANY real XFCE/desktop-shell content has ever been confirmed rendering on screen in this project's entire multi-session documented history (every prior entry in this file, across dozens of sub-sessions, reports solid black or, at best, a compositor's own built-in fallback background color).

**Committed** (`e72f7389`, authored as `lanmower`): the identity fix, re-applied byte-for-byte from its original `eeaec7c2` diff (see that commit's own message for the full original rationale), verified live this time with the combination of fixes this session has accumulated.

**Current state, precisely**: the panel/clock area renders and updates correctly; the REST of the desktop (background image/color, full-width panel, taskbar icons, desktop icons) does not yet appear -- the window is still mostly solid black outside the small panel area visible in the top-left/top-right. This is real, substantial, partial progress toward the standing goal, not yet the "complete, perfect display" the standing instruction requires. **Follow-up this same pass: the clock/panel rendering is NON-DETERMINISTIC, not a guaranteed "always works" state**. Tried three further variants: (a) the FULL canonical `xfce_launch.sh` script (D-Bus, `xfsettingsd`, `xfce4-panel`, `xfdesktop` all together) -- died silently very early (~7s, before any progress marker), twice; (b) bare weston + `xfdesktop` added (no `--xwayland`, `WAYLAND_DISPLAY` only) -- completed cleanly, zero crashes, but `xfdesktop` itself failed with `cannot open display:` (needs `DISPLAY`, not `WAYLAND_DISPLAY`, for its X11/XWayland connection) and no panel/clock appeared either; (c) bare weston + `xfdesktop` + `--xwayland` + `DISPLAY=:0` -- completed with one secondary-process `SIGSEGV` (`pid=6`, not the main weston/desktop-shell process), window showed the same benign white/black host-window-sizing artifact identified earlier this session, no clock. **Then re-ran the EXACT original bare-weston-only configuration that first showed the live clock** (identical command, same tars, same fix in place) -- process ran cleanly, zero crashes, desktop-shell launched (confirmed via log, same 379-line count as the successful run), but this time the screenshot showed NO clock/panel at all, solid black. **This confirms the breakthrough is real and the process is now genuinely, reliably crash-free with this fix in place, but the actual visible rendering remains non-deterministic** -- consistent with this whole bug family's own well-documented flaky/heisenbug nature throughout this session and prior ones. **CORRECTED this same pass -- it is NOT non-deterministic, it is a TIMING issue: waiting longer reliably reproduces it FOR THE BARE-WESTON CONFIGURATION.** All of the "failed" screenshots above were taken after only ~20-30 real seconds. Re-ran the identical bare-weston command and waited a full 85 real seconds (not ~25s) before screenshotting: **the clock reappeared** (`.wfgy/xfce-build/longwait_screenshot.png`, "Mon Aug 31, 08:03 PM"), with zero crashes across the whole run. This means the panel genuinely, reliably populates given enough real time for the MINIMAL bare-weston repro -- **not a heisenbug for that specific configuration.**

**FOUND AND FIXED the actual crash this same pass -- a real, simple, concrete bug, not a deep architectural issue**: captured a debug trace of the crash and found the EXACT cause: `[HH:MM:SS] failed to bind to /tmp/.X11-unix/X0: No such file` -- weston's own XWayland startup fails because `/tmp/.X11-unix/` doesn't exist, immediately followed by a SIGSEGV in the MAIN process (`pid=1000`, the same `sh`/`weston` guest identity this file has tracked throughout, NOT a secondary `Xwayland` child as earlier entries in this file guessed from less complete evidence -- weston's own error-handling path for this specific bind failure appears to crash rather than gracefully report the error). The canonical `xfce_launch.sh` script (referenced at the very top of this file) already creates this directory (`mkdir -p /tmp/.X11-unix; chmod 1777 /tmp/.X11-unix`) -- this pass's own simplified test scripts had dropped that line, reintroducing a bug the project's own canonical repro had already solved long ago.

**Fixed and verified**: added `mkdir -p /tmp/.X11-unix; chmod 1777 /tmp/.X11-unix` back into the test script, re-ran the FULL script (weston with `--xwayland`, 60s warmup, then `xfdesktop`) -- **zero crashes across the entire run**, script completed fully, and a real screenshot (`.wfgy/xfce-build/fixed_x11dir_screenshot.png`) shows the SAME live, correctly-updating panel/clock ("Mon Aug 31, 08:14 PM") as the bare-weston breakthrough, this time with the FULL script (including the `xfdesktop` launch attempt) running throughout with no crash at all. `xfdesktop` itself still shows `cannot open display: :0` (XWayland's own `:0` socket still isn't ready/available to it even after the crash fix and 60s wait -- a separate, narrower, now-isolated remaining issue, not the crash), so the desktop BACKGROUND still doesn't appear, but the crash that was blocking ALL full-stack progress is now genuinely resolved.

**Concrete next step, narrowly scoped**: with the crash fixed, the remaining gap is specifically why XWayland's own `:0` X11 socket isn't ready/reachable for `xfdesktop` even ~60+ seconds after weston (with `--xwayland`) starts -- check whether XWayland needs its OWN explicit readiness wait (polling for `/tmp/.X11-unix/X0` itself, mirroring the existing Wayland-socket poll loop pattern already used elsewhere in this file's own scripts) rather than assuming weston's own overall startup time is sufficient, since XWayland is a separate, lazily-spawned process within weston (per this file's own earlier, real-source-verified finding on XWayland's lazy-spawn-on-first-connection-attempt behavior) that may need its own dedicated wait condition.

# STATUS (2026-08-31, resolved a brief false lead about desktop-shell's own parent tid -- no anomaly, litebox's guest-pid model is correct): noticed `weston-desktop-shell`'s own `clone: spawned new task` log line shows `parent_tid=1000` (the SAME guest tid as the original `sh`), not weston's own tid, and briefly suspected a real attribution bug (`self.tid` misreporting the caller). Checked weston's own `sys_execve: entry` line and found it ALSO shows `tid=1000` -- meaning `weston --backend=... &` in the launch script did not fork a new child at all; it tail-`execve`'d IN PLACE of `sh`'s own process image (a real, correct, common shell behavior for how busybox `ash` can handle a background job in certain script shapes), so `sh`'s own guest tid 1000 simply BECAME weston's identity from that point on -- desktop-shell's `parent_tid=1000` is therefore fully correct and expected, matching real Linux `execve()`'s own PID-preservation semantics, not a litebox bug or a new lead. **On reflection this actually sharpens, rather than dismisses, the "long-lived parent accumulates corruption" hypothesis**: since `weston` and the original `sh` are the SAME guest tid (1000) via a tail-`execve`, the process that later forks `weston-desktop-shell` (tid=15) has been CONTINUOUSLY ALIVE since the very start of the whole script -- through `seatd`'s own launch, weston's own `execve`, and however many `mmap`/`fork`/syscalls weston's own real startup performs -- before it ever forks desktop-shell. This means the "long-lived parent" in this theory is not some abstract concept; it is concretely, precisely, THIS ONE process (guest tid 1000, real host tid whatever `weston`'s own `sys_execve` reported), continuously alive and accumulating real memory/state history the entire time, right up until the fork that produces desktop-shell. This is a MORE concrete, more mechanistically plausible version of the standing hypothesis than "sh watches its children fork" -- it is specifically "does litebox's own guest-process bookkeeping (`CURRENT_GUEST_PID`, `CLAIMED_RANGES`, or something else entirely) hold any STALE data tied to this tid's PREVIOUS identity (as plain `sh`, or as `seatd`'s own launcher) that outlives the `execve` into `weston` and corrupts weston's OWN later fork of desktop-shell" -- a real, specific, checkable question for whoever continues this investigation with working live-debugging tools, sharper than anything this pass identified before it.

# STATUS (2026-08-31, ruled out three more concrete resource-leak hypotheses for the "long-lived parent accumulates corruption" theory -- none panned out, all checked against real code/trace evidence): followed up the corrected "sh accumulates corruption from watching its own children fork" hypothesis with three more concrete, checkable candidate mechanisms. (1) `CLAIMED_RANGES`'s `MAX_CLAIMS`-eviction (2048-slot registry, LRU-evicts the oldest claim when full) -- re-read `find_foreign_claim`'s own logic and confirmed eviction only ever REMOVES the registry's own ability to detect a FUTURE collision with that specific range; it cannot itself corrupt anything retroactively, so this isn't a plausible corruption SOURCE, only a (already-understood, already-documented) detection-coverage gap. (2) A genuine Windows thread-stack memory leak from litebox's own code across many `fork()`s -- checked every `VirtualFree`/`CloseHandle` call site in `litebox_platform_windows_userland/src/lib.rs`; all handle real GUEST-managed memory (`allocate_pages`'s own mmap regions), not the real Windows OS thread's own stack, which `std::thread::Builder`/Rust's own runtime already manages automatically on `JoinHandle` teardown -- no manual leak found in litebox's own code for this. (3) A genuine thread-count/handle leak (threads spawned but never detached) -- counted `clone: spawned new task` (14) vs `detach_thread: decremented nr_threads` (12) in this session's own earlier full trace; the 2-thread gap is fully explained by weston+desktop-shell still being alive when that trace was captured (expected, not a leak), and 14 total threads across a whole session is far too small a number to plausibly exhaust process address space or any other bounded resource on its own. **None of these three checkable candidate mechanisms explain the corruption** -- the "long-lived parent process accumulates corruption from watching its own children's fork/exec cycles" framing remains the best available lead, but ITS OWN specific mechanism is still unidentified after this pass's exhaustive check of the most obvious, checkable candidates. Given the depth of investigation already invested this pass without a safely-testable next hypothesis remaining, and this project's own extensive history of this exact bug resisting quick fixes across many prior sessions, further progress on this specific bug genuinely requires live process debugging tooling (a debugger attach, or a custom instrumented build) that this pass could not get working (see the `LD_PRELOAD`-interposition-doesn't-work-under-tcc/musl entry above) -- concluding this specific investigative thread here rather than attempting an uninformed, high-risk patch to code this project's own history shows is unusually easy to make WORSE, not better, without solid live evidence first (see this file's own "guest-pid identity fix" revert, much earlier in this same session, for a directly analogous cautionary precedent).

# STATUS (2026-08-31, LIKELY ARCHITECTURAL EXPLANATION FOUND for why the trigger is specifically "late execve": `fork_verify`'s protection window is tied to `sh`'s OWN, single, very-early post-fork period, and is permanently torn down the moment `sh` itself calls `execve` -- meaning it protects almost none of a real shell's actual working lifetime): re-read `fork_verify.rs`'s own `begin()`/`end()` wiring against `sys_execve` (`litebox_shim_linux/src/syscalls/process.rs` line 4130, already read earlier this session) and confirmed precisely: `begin_fork_child_verification()` (process.rs line 4438) arms verification ONLY in response to a genuine `fork()`/`clone()` syscall, and `end_fork_child_verification()` (process.rs lines 1802, 1808, 4130) tears it down -- critically, `sys_execve` itself calls `end_fork_child_verification()` UNCONDITIONALLY, every time, including for a process's OWN self-`execve` (e.g. the interactive `sh` in this whole repro's own launch chain, which is itself `execve`'d once very early from whatever spawned it). **This means `fork_verify`'s entire protection window for a long-lived shell process like `sh` is only its OWN brief post-fork-before-first-execve period** -- the instant `sh` calls its own first `execve` (replacing itself, standard shell startup), verification is permanently, irrevocably torn down for that process/thread for the REST of its lifetime, no matter how many children it later forks (`seatd &`, `weston &`, `apk`'s internal forks, `tcc`, `/tmp/racetest`) -- each of THOSE children gets ITS OWN fresh, bounded verification window at ITS OWN fork point, but `sh` ITSELF, the long-lived PARENT accumulating potentially-stale pointers across its entire multi-minute, many-syscall lifetime, has had ZERO fork_verify protection since its own startup. **This is a fully plausible, architecturally-clean explanation for exactly the "late execve corrupts, early execve doesn't" pattern this pass found**: any stale-pointer corruption `sh` itself accumulates from watching its OWN children fork (each child fork potentially leaves stale references in the PARENT's own memory too, not just the child's, if any shared/CoW state is involved) has no mechanism to ever get healed, and simply accumulates, unaddressed, until it finally corrupts something `sh` touches during its own next `execve` call late in its life. **Checked one more piece before finalizing this hypothesis, and it needed a real correction**: verified whether `sh` (the root shell driving this whole repro, guest pid/tid 1000) was ever itself a fork CHILD at all -- searched this session's own earlier full trace for every `clone: spawned new task` line and found `parent_tid=1000` on every single one, with `sh`/tid=1000 never once appearing as a `child_tid`. This confirms `sh` is litebox's own INIT/ROOT guest process (launched directly by the runner via `run_thread`, not via any guest-level `fork()`/`clone()` at all -- consistent with this session's own earlier reading of `litebox_runner_linux_on_windows_userland/src/lib.rs`'s `run()` function). So `sh` was NEVER a `fork_verify`-protected fork child in the first place, for its entire lifetime, by design -- not "protected briefly then abandoned" as the paragraph above first framed it, but "never in scope for this mechanism at all," which is actually the CORRECT, intentional behavior for a process that was never forked (there is no "stale post-fork pointer" risk for a process that never went through a `fork()` itself). **This means the actual corruption source, whatever it is, must come from something ELSE -- most plausibly a side-effect `sh` experiences purely from repeatedly WATCHING/OWNING many child forks over its long lifetime** (e.g. some shared bookkeeping structure, a `CLAIMED_RANGES`-adjacent registry, or real CoW/shared-memory state that accumulates staleness in the PARENT as a consequence of the CHILDREN's own fork/exec cycles, not from `sh`'s own non-existent fork event) -- a genuinely different, not-yet-precisely-identified mechanism than `fork_verify`'s own single-step healing was ever designed to address, since that machinery's whole contract is scoped to "this specific process was just forked," which never applied to `sh` here. **Not fixed this pass** -- but this corrected, more precise framing (the bug is NOT a `fork_verify` window-expiry gap, but a genuinely unexplained "long-lived parent process accumulates corruption from watching its own children's fork/exec cycles" effect) is a materially better, more falsifiable starting point for the `npx casey` investigation than the "expired verification window" theory this same entry first proposed a few paragraphs above, before this correction.

Then ran `apk add wayland-dev tcc musl-dev` AFTER `weston &` was already stably backgrounded and running (not before, as every previous attempt this pass did) -- this ALSO completed cleanly, install succeeded, `tcc` compiled a fresh binary successfully, all while weston kept running in the background with zero crashes across a full 60s window. **This isolates the actual trigger with much more precision than previously achieved**: it is not `weston`'s own fork, not `apk`'s own internal forks (busybox/tar extraction), not `tcc` compiling -- it is specifically running `/tmp/racetest` (the freshly-compiled, freshly-`execve`'d TEST CLIENT itself) immediately after that, which silently kills the whole process tree with zero crash markers, zero further output, matching the SAME exact silent-death signature this session found for `weston-simple-shm`, `weston-smoke`, and `weston-flower` earlier. **This is now the SEVENTH distinct real binary** (five weston demo clients, `cc1`/`gcc`, and now this session's own minimal hand-written test client) that hits this exact bug the moment it tries to `execve` as a genuinely fresh client process late in an already-busy, already-fork-heavy XFCE-shaped process tree -- overwhelming, multi-angle, cross-binary confirmation that this is a real, general, severe bug affecting ANY late client launch in this specific scenario, not something specific to any one binary's own code. The registry-ordering race theory (this file's own earlier, source-verified finding) remains the best explanation for WHY desktop-shell specifically never draws, but this session's own repeated, careful attempts to verify it live have now conclusively shown that doing so is blocked by this separate, even-more-fundamental corruption bug -- which itself is very likely ALSO the true root cause of desktop-shell's own stall (not just an obstacle to studying it), since desktop-shell's own helper client is ALSO exactly this shape: a fresh `execve` launched late, deep in an already-busy weston process tree.

# STATUS (2026-08-31, NEW, SMALL, DETERMINISTIC repro found for the standing mallocng/fork-corruption bug -- `sh`'s OWN argv parsing corrupted by a sibling `fork()`): while building a minimal standalone Wayland client to test the registry-ordering race directly (using this pass's own newly-working `tcc` toolchain), hit a genuinely new, small, 100%-DETERMINISTIC repro of the project's standing corruption bug. The script `seatd -l debug & sleep 3; weston --backend=drm-backend.so --use-pixman & sleep 8; ...` fails EVERY time (2/2 fresh runs, byte-identical) with `sleep: invalid number '8�F'` -- the literal argument `8` to the SECOND `sleep` call (immediately after `weston &`, a `fork()`) arrives corrupted with trailing garbage bytes, while the FIRST `sleep 3` (right after `seatd &`, an earlier `fork()`) is unaffected. This means **`sh`'s own process memory -- specifically, whatever buffer holds its next command's argv string -- is being corrupted as a side effect of the `weston &` fork operation itself**, not a crash in the child (`weston` launches and runs fine afterward in earlier tests) and not something wrong with `sleep` or the script's own authoring (verified byte-for-byte correct via direct `sed` inspection of the source file). This is a NEW manifestation of the already-documented `fork_verify`/mallocng corruption bug family (previously seen as heap-corruption SIGILL crashes in freshly-`execve`'d children; this is instead argv-buffer corruption in the surviving PARENT shell after a sibling fork) -- and, being small, deterministic, and NOT requiring XFCE/weston/DRM at all to reproduce (should be checked whether a much simpler `sh -c 'sleep 1 & sleep 2'`-shaped script reproduces it too), this may be a significantly more tractable repro for the standing `npx casey` investigation than anything found before. **Attempted two quick minimization checks this pass**: (1) a bare `sh -c "/bin/true & sleep 1; /bin/true & sleep 2"` (no weston at all) -- clean, does NOT reproduce; (2) `weston --help` (a fast, minimal weston invocation, no DRM/compositor init) followed by `sleep 8` -- also clean, does NOT reproduce. This means the corruption specifically needs the HEAVY, real `weston --backend=drm-backend.so --use-pixman` launch (genuine DRM/compositor/thread init), not just any `fork()` or any `weston` invocation -- narrowing it somewhat, but still requiring the fuller apk+tcc+weston combination to reproduce, so it is NOT as trivially small as initially hoped. **Not investigated further this pass** (time-boxed after these two negative results) -- recommended as a next repro to hand to the standing `npx casey` investigation regardless, since it's still meaningfully smaller/faster than the full multi-tar XFCE repro (no XFCE layer, no seatd dependency even required per the `weston --help` test, just a heavy-enough weston launch) and gives a concrete, deterministic, byte-identical corruption signature (`'8\xNN F'`-shaped argv corruption) to correlate against `fork_verify`'s own heal log, exactly the kind of hard evidence that investigation's own prior entries have repeatedly asked for.

# STATUS (2026-08-31, `tcc` compile blocker GENUINELY SOLVED this pass -- but LD_PRELOAD function-interposition on weston itself does NOT work under this musl/tcc combination, a real, separate limitation): found and fixed the `tcc` hang from the immediately-following entry -- it was `apk`'s own post-install hook scripts (the `busybox-1.37.0-r31.trigger` step), not `tcc` itself; `apk add --no-cache --no-scripts tcc musl-dev` (skipping hook scripts entirely, which this specific package set doesn't need) installs cleanly and fast every time. **Successfully compiled a real, working `.so` with `tcc`** (`tcc -B<dir> -shared -fPIC -o out.so in.c`, where `<dir>` contains a valid-but-empty `libtcc1.a` -- a bare `!<arch>\n` ar-format magic header, since Alpine's `tcc` package doesn't ship the real one and none of the runtime helpers it would provide are needed for simple x86_64 code) -- this **genuinely, fully solves the standing `cc1`/DLL-collision compile blocker** for any future diagnostic tool that doesn't need gcc-specific extensions: `tcc` never goes anywhere near the `ET_EXEC`/DLL-collision address range `cc1` does.

**However, `LD_PRELOAD` function-interposition on `weston_output_finish_frame` (or any libweston-internal function) does not work in this environment**, confirmed across FIVE distinct variants this pass: (1) `dlsym(RTLD_NEXT, ...)` called from a `__attribute__((constructor))`, which fires but the `dlsym` call itself never returns/logs its result; (2) the same `dlsym` moved to lazy, first-call resolution instead -- the override function itself is never even entered, meaning weston's own internal calls to `weston_output_finish_frame` never route through my `.so`'s definition at all; (3) an explicit `dlopen("libweston-14.so.0", RTLD_NOW|RTLD_NOLOAD)` + `dlsym(handle, ...)` instead of `RTLD_NEXT` -- same result, override never entered; (4) a `wl_proxy_marshal_flags` interceptor (would have caught EVERY Wayland protocol request generically, sidestepping the specific-function question) -- failed to even compile under `tcc` due to varargs-forwarding limitations (`tcc`'s libc/ABI doesn't support the `va_list`-to-`...`-forwarding trick this needed); (5) confirmed a MUCH simpler, unrelated interposition target (`malloc`, called extremely early and frequently) actively SEGFAULTS when overridden this way, suggesting `dlsym(RTLD_NEXT, ...)`-based interposition has a genuine, deeper compatibility problem specific to this musl+tcc combination (possibly musl's own dynamic linker resolving weston's internal calls via a more direct/eager binding than glibc's traditionally-permissive lazy PLT resolution, which is what makes `LD_PRELOAD` overriding work AT ALL on typical glibc-based Alpine-adjacent or Ubuntu-style systems) -- confirmed a plain `__attribute__((constructor))`-only shim (no interposition, just "print something when loaded") DOES work correctly and load into weston successfully, isolating the failure specifically to the interposition mechanism, not `LD_PRELOAD`/`tcc`-produced-`.so`-loading in general.

**Where this leaves things**: the `cc1` compile blocker is now solved (a real, durable, reusable win for any FUTURE diagnostic need in this environment), but `LD_PRELOAD`-based function tracing specifically does not work here, so it cannot be the mechanism for directly instrumenting `weston_output_finish_frame`/`output_init`/`main()`'s catch-up loop as this file's own prior entries hoped. **Concrete next step for whoever continues, now that `tcc` genuinely works**: a debug-instrumented REBUILD of `weston-desktop-shell` itself (inserting `fprintf` calls directly into the SOURCE before compiling, rather than trying to intercept an already-built binary externally) is now achievable -- fetch `clients/desktop-shell.c` from the already-proven-working GitHub mirror (`gh api repos/wayland-mirror/weston/contents/clients/desktop-shell.c?ref=14.0.1`), add `fprintf(stderr, "[DIAG] ...")` lines directly into `create_output`/`output_init`/`main()`'s catch-up loop, then compile with `tcc` using this pass's own proven-working recipe (`--no-scripts`, dummy `libtcc1.a`) -- linking against the REAL `libwayland-client`/`weston-desktop-shell` toolkit libraries already installed in the rootfs (not reimplementing them), which `tcc`'s straightforward C compilation should handle correctly since the earlier failures were specifically about POST-hoc interposition of an existing binary, not about compiling and linking fresh C code against existing shared libraries (which this pass's own successful `trace.so`/`trace7.so`/`trace8.so` compiles already proved works fine).
